# Discretisation and independent resolutions of ample groupoids

**arXiv ID**: 2606.21761v1
**Authors**: Xin Li, Alistair Miller
**Published**: 2026-06-19
**Categories**: math.KT, math.OA
**Comments**: 54 pages
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2606.21761v1

## Abstract

We develop a general framework for understanding and computing both the groupoid homology of an ample groupoid and the topological K-theory of its reduced C*-algebra, based on two main ideas: discretisation and independent resolutions. Discretisation shows that a special class of ample groupoids we term independent groupoids are homologically and K-theoretically equivalent to discrete groupoids. We introduce the notion of a resolution by independent groupoids and provide a recipe for building a controlled independent resolution of a given ample groupoid of interest, leading to a systematic way of studying its homology and K-theory. In order to illustrate our general ideas and methods, we work out several concrete examples and applications. Garside categories provide a wide range of examples, including higher rank graphs, self-similar groups and spherical Artin-Tits groups. We also present an application to the homology of Stein's groups.

Discretisation and independent resolutions of ample groupoids

## Abstract.

We develop a general framework for understanding and computing both the groupoid homology of an ample groupoid and the topological K-theory of its reducedC∗\mathrm{C}^{\ast}-algebra, based on two main ideas: discretisation and independent resolutions. Discretisation shows that a special class of ample groupoids we termindependentgroupoids are homologically and K-theoretically equivalent to discrete groupoids.
We introduce the notion of aresolutionby independent groupoids and provide a recipe for building a controlled independent resolution of a given ample groupoid of interest, leading to a systematic way of studying its homology and K-theory.
In order to illustrate our general ideas and methods, we work out several concrete examples and applications. Garside categories provide a wide range of examples, including higher rank graphs, self-similar groups and spherical Artin–Tits groups. We also present an application to the homology of Stein’s groups.

## 1.Introduction

Topological K-theory is an invariant forC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras which plays an important role in Elliott’s classification programme[26,27,25,77,75,9]as well as non-commutative geometry and its applications to topology and geometry in the context of the Baum–Connes conjecture[3,73]. The goal of the present paper is to develop a general framework which allows us to study and compute this fundamental invariant forC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras of ample groupoids, which is a rich class ofC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras covering prominent examples such as AF algebras, Kirchberg algebras satisfying the UCT, Cuntz–Krieger algebras, graphC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras, higher rank graphC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras, semigroupC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras andC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras attached to self-similar groups. Our methods also provide an approach to understanding and determining groupoid homology as introduced in[13], which is a fundamental invariant for ample groupoids that has been used successfully to classify topological dynamical systems[24,22,23]and exhibits interesting connections to the group homology of topological full groups[40,41,42,67,37]. The latter is a class of groups of dynamical origin with striking properties[30,54,63].

Our framework is based on two main ingredients: discretisation and independent resolutions.

The idea of discretisation in K-theory goes back to[14], where the initial motivation was to compute K-theory for certain semigroupC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras. Based on[14], a conceptual perspective involving topological dynamical systems was developed in[15], generalizing K-theory formulas obtained in[14]to crossed products of certain group actions on totally disconnected spaces. An alternative perspective using the language of inverse semigroups and theirC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras emerged from[55]. Both perspectives have then been further developed in[34], which established discretisation for crossed products of certain partial group actions. More recently,[50]developed discretisation for more general groupoids without relying on the presence of partial group actions, but assuming that the groupoids of interest have torsion-free isotropy groups. In the algebraic context, discretisation has been formulated in[2].

Independent resolutions were first introduced in[38,39]. Their purpose is to describe general ample groupoids in terms of groupoids which are covered by discretisation, enabling us to study and compute K-theory and groupoid homology.

## Theorem A(Discretisation in K-theory, see Theorem4.12).

LetGGbe the universal groupoid of a countable inverse semigroupSSwith idempotent semilatticeEEsuch thatGGis Hausdorff and satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture. Consider the action ofSSonE×=E∖{0}E^{\times}=E\setminus\{0\}as a discrete space and assume further that the transformation groupoidS⋉E×S\ltimes E^{\times}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture. ThenK∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(G))≅K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(S⋉E×)).\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(G))\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes E^{\times})).

This reduces the K-theory computation forGGto that of the discrete groupoidS⋉E×S\ltimes E^{\times}. The computation breaks down further by Morita equivalence invariance to a K-theory computation for the isotropy groups of the discrete groupoid. Explicitly, in this case we obtainK∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(G))≅⨁[e]∈S\E×K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(Se)),\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(G))\cong\bigoplus_{[e]\in S\backslash E^{\times}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S_{e})),

whereSe={s∈S∣s∗​s=s​s∗=e}S_{e}=\{s\in S\mid s^{*}s=ss^{*}=e\}is the maximal subgroup at a non-zero idempotente∈E×e\in E^{\times}. Note thatS⋉E×S\ltimes E^{\times}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture if and only ifSeS_{e}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture for eache∈E×e\in E^{\times}.

It is worth pointing out that the K-theoretic isomorphism in TheoremAis induced by a concrete étale groupoid correspondence (see Definitions4.1and4.4).

Previous work only established this discretisation formula for special inverse semigroups (strongly0-EE-unitary ones, see[34]) or needed the extra assumption that the isotropy groups ofGGare torsion-free (see[50]). In contrast, our discretisation result (Theorem4.12) is close to the optimal level of generality. The Baum–Connes conjecture is used because in the actual proof, an isomorphism is constructed for the left-hand side of the Baum–Connes conjecture (the reader may consult[71,72,57,7,58]and the references therein for more information about the Baum–Connes conjecture for groupoids). This explains the assumptions “countable” and “Hausdorff”, because there is currently no suitable formulation of the Baum–Connes conjecture available without these hypotheses. For example, if a second countable Hausdorff étale groupoidGGis amenable or has the Haagerup property, then the Baum–Connes conjecture is valid forGGby[71]. We note however that discretisation is still natural and desirable in the non-Hausdorff setting, and is known to hold for some examples (such as in the setting of Example6.10as used in[51]).

To apply TheoremAin practical circumstances it is useful to be able to recognise when an ample groupoidGGcan be realised as the universal groupoid of an inverse semigroup, and what data one should have to perform this realisation. SupposeG=S⋉XG=S\ltimes Xfor some inverse semigroup actionS↷XS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}X. A regular basis[15, §2]is a set𝒰\mathcal{U}of non-empty compact open sets inXXsuch that𝒰∪{∅}\mathcal{U}\cup\{\emptyset\}is closed under intersection, generates the topology ofXXin a suitable sense and satisfies theindependencecondition: ifU,U1,…,Un∈𝒰U,U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\in\mathcal{U}satisfyU⊆⋃i=1nUiU\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}, thenU⊆UiU\subseteq U_{i}for someii.
IfXXadmits anSS-invariant regular basis, thenG=S⋉XG=S\ltimes Xmay be identified with the universal groupoid of an inverse semigroup. Thus TheoremAadmits the following dynamical reformulation.

## Theorem A′(Discretisation in K-theory, a dynamical perspective).

SupposeS↷XS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Xis an action of a countable inverse semigroupSSon a second countable totally disconnected locally compact Hausdorff spaceXXsuch thatG=S⋉XG=S\ltimes Xis Hausdorff and satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture. Suppose thatXXadmits anSS-invariant regular basis𝒰\mathcal{U}of compact open sets. Consider the action ofSSon𝒰\mathcal{U}as a discrete space and suppose that for eachU∈𝒰U\in\mathcal{U}the isotropy groupSU=(S⋉𝒰)UUS_{U}=(S\ltimes\mathcal{U})^{U}_{U}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture. ThenK∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(G))≅K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(S⋉𝒰))≅⨁[U]∈S\𝒰K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(SU)).\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(G))\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes\mathcal{U}))\cong\bigoplus_{[U]\in S\backslash\mathcal{U}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S_{U})).

Groupoids which admit anSS-invariant regular basis are examples of a class we termindependentgroupoids (see Definition2.21). However, this is a very special class of groupoids, and an ample groupoidGGof interest will likely not be independent. Instead, we look for aresolutionofGGby independent groupoids. This is an “exact” sequence of independent groupoids(Gk=Sk⋉Xk)k≥0(G_{k}=S_{k}\ltimes X_{k})_{k\geq 0}⋯→Gk→⋯→G0→G→∅,\cdots\to G_{k}\to\cdots\to G_{0}\to G\to\emptyset,

i.e. we have closed invariant subsetsCk⊆XkC_{k}\subseteq X_{k}together with isomorphismsG≅G0|C0G\cong G_{0}|_{C_{0}},Gk|Xk∖Ck≅Gk+1|Ck+1G_{k}|_{X_{k}\setminus C_{k}}\cong G_{k+1}|_{C_{k+1}}(see Definition3.1). Under additional exactness assumptions, an independent resolution produces an exact sequence at the level of reduced groupoidC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras. When the resolution has finite length (i.e. eventuallyGk=∅G_{k}=\emptyset), a series of six-term exact sequences relates the K-theory ofGGto that of the independent groupoidsGkG_{k}, which can be computed through TheoremA. Independent resolutions always exist for abstract reasons, but this does not guarantee finite length resolutions.

Dynamical systemsS↷XS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Xmay often be studied through a naturally occurringSS-invariant collection of compact open subsets inXX. Arepresentation(see Definition2.10) of a semilatticeEEonXXis a homomorphismU:E→𝒪c​(X)U\colon E\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)

sending0to∅\emptysetwhich realises eache∈Ee\in Eas a compact open setU​(e)U(e)inXX. We consider representationsU:E→𝒪c​(X)U\colon E\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)of semilatticesEEequipped with an action ofSSby partial order automorphisms (see Definition2.17) such thatUUisSS-equivariant; ifUUis faithful and its image isSS-invariant,EEwill inherit such an action. It is reasonable to hope that one’s favourite dynamical systemS↷XS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Xadmits a naturally occurringSS-equivariant representationU:E→𝒪c​(X)U\colon E\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)of a semilatticeEEwhich generates the set of compact open sets inXX(we sayUUisgenerating), but in many casesUUwill fail the following independence condition:(1.1)ife,e1,…,en∈Ee,e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\in EsatisfyU​(e)⊆⋃i=1nU​(ei)U(e)\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U(e_{i}), thene≤eie\leq e_{i}for someii.

The key idea that we exploit to build independent resolutions is that in many natural examples, the independence condition (1.1) will fail in a systematic way. Aconcrete finite coverofe∈Ee\in Eis a finite setF⊆EF\subseteq EbeloweewithU​(e)=⋃f∈FU​(f)U(e)=\bigcup_{f\in F}U(f). Each failure of the independence condition is witnessed by a (non-trivial) concrete finite cover. We considersystemsℛ={ℛ​(e)∣e∈E},\mathscr{R}=\{\mathscr{R}(e)\mid e\in E\},

of finite covers, where eachℛ​(e)\mathscr{R}(e)is a set of concrete finite covers ofee(see Definition3.6). It turns out that anSS-invariant systemℛ\mathscr{R}which is large enough to explain every failure of independence (we sayℛ\mathscr{R}isthorough, see Definition3.7) contains enough data to explicitly construct an independent resolution ofG=S⋉XG=S\ltimes X. This gives us control over the independent resolution in terms of the systemℛ\mathscr{R}of finite covers, and in particular enables us to construct finite length independent resolutions.

## Theorem B(Independent resolutions, see Theorem3.15).

LetG=S⋉XG=S\ltimes Xbe an ample groupoid and letU:E→𝒪c​(X)U\colon E\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)be anSS-equivariant generating representation of a semilatticeEEby compact open subsets inXX. Letℛ\mathscr{R}be a thoroughSS-invariant system of concrete finite covers inEEwith respect toUU. Then there is an independent resolution⋯→Gk→⋯→G0→G→∅\cdots\to G_{k}\to\cdots\to G_{0}\to G\to\emptyset

ofGGsuch that for eachk≥0k\geq 0,Gk=S⋉XkG_{k}=S\ltimes X_{k}for anSS-spaceXkX_{k}admitting anSS-invariant regular basis, andGk+1=∅G_{k+1}=\emptysetfork≥supe∈E#​{F∈ℛ​(e)∣F​non-trivial}k\geq\sup_{e\in E}\#\{F\in\mathscr{R}(e)\mid F\text{ non-trivial}\}.

For groupoids of source-free row-finite higher rank graphs (see[31]and also[59,60]), we always obtain finite length independent resolutions, see Example3.16. Combined with discretisation, our work provides an alternative perspective (see Example6.9) on K-theory computations carried out in[20]. For groupoids attached to self-similar groups, K-theory computations have been worked out in[52,51]. In this case, we obtain an independent resolution of length11(see Example6.10), which combined with discretisation leads to a conceptual explanation of the results in[52,51]. We note that higher rank graphs only fit into the precise setup of TheoremBif they are singly aligned. Following[47, § 8], to treat a more general finitely aligned setup, we replace semilattices with a more general notion oflocally finite weak semilattices(see §2.1). TheoremsA,A′andBare all established in the context of locally finite weak semilattices in the main text.

We highlight that TheoremBnot only helps us to understand ample groupoids, but also discrete groups. AnArtin–Tits groupΓ\Gammais a group given by generatorsSSand relationsRRof the forms​t​s​t​⋯⏟ms,telements=t​s​t​s​⋯⏟ms,telements\underbrace{stst\cdots}_{\text{$m_{s,t}$ elements}}=\underbrace{tsts\cdots}_{\text{$m_{s,t}$ elements}}

The monoidMΓ=⟨S∣R⟩+M_{\Gamma}=\langle S\mid R\rangle^{+}with the same presentation is the correspondingArtin–Tits monoid. The Artin–Tits groupΓ\Gammais said to besphericalif the quotient group (theCoxeter group) determined by the relationss2=1s^{2}=1is finite. In this caseMΓM_{\Gamma}embeds inΓ\Gamma. This gives rise to the semilatticeE0:-{γ​MΓ|γ∈Γ}∪{∅}E_{0}\coloneq\left\{\gamma M_{\Gamma}\;|\;\gamma\in\Gamma\right\}\cup\{\emptyset\}of subsets ofΓ\Gamma, where the partial order is given by inclusion.Γ\Gammaacts onE0E_{0}by order automorphisms via left multiplication. TheoremBapplies to the trivial representation ofE0E_{0}on the trivialΓ\Gamma-space{∗}\{*\}together with a naturally constructed systemℛ\mathscr{R}of finite covers, yielding a finite length independent resolution ofΓ=Γ⋉{∗}\Gamma=\Gamma\ltimes\{*\}with trivial isotropy groups. We obtain the following corollary.

We say thediscretisation formula with arbitrary coefficientsis valid for a countable discrete groupΓ\Gammaif for every countable semilatticeEE, everyΓ\Gamma-actionΓ↷E\Gamma\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Eby order automorphisms and every separableΓ\Gamma-C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebraAA, there is an isomorphismK∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(E×)))≅K∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(E^))).\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(E^{\times})))\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(\widehat{E}))).

HereE^\widehat{E}is the character space ofEEandΓ\Gammaacts diagonally onA⊗C0​(E×)A\otimes C_{0}(E^{\times})andA⊗C0​(E^)A\otimes C_{0}(\widehat{E}).

## Corollary C(see Corollary6.11and Example6.12).

LetΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}be a subgroup of a spherical Artin–Tits group. ThenΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture with arbitrary coefficients if and only if the discretisation formula with arbitrary coefficients is valid forΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}.

Actually, we prove this result in the more general case of exact subgroups of enveloping groups of Garside monoids (Corollary6.11).
Note that Braid groups — a particular case of spherical Artin–Tits groups — are shown to satisfy the Baum–Connes conjecture with arbitrary coefficients in[62]. However, the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficients is open for general spherical Artin–Tits groups. (For related results on the Baum–Connes conjecture for Artin–Tits groups, see[29]and the references therein.)

All of the examples mentioned so far are particular cases of groupoids attached to Garside categories (see Definitions6.7and6.8) as in[35,36]. For these groupoids, we obtain finite length independent resolutions as soon as the underlying Garside category satisfies appropriate finiteness conditions. In this way, our work provides a unifying perspective on all the examples above.

Let us turn to discretisation in groupoid homology. In this setting, unlike for K-theory, no extra hypotheses (such as satisfaction of the Baum–Connes conjecture, Hausdorffness, or finiteness requirements) are needed, and no exactness is required in applications of independent resolutions. The following discretisation formula is proved in[49, Example 3.10].

## Theorem D(Discretisation in homology).

LetGGbe the universal groupoid of a countable inverse semigroupSSwith idempotent semilatticeEE. ThenH∗⁡(G)≅H∗⁡(S⋉E×)≅⨁[e]∈S\E×H∗⁡(Se),\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(G)\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes E^{\times})\cong\bigoplus_{[e]\in S\backslash E^{\times}}\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S_{e}),

whereSe={s∈S∣s∗​s=s​s∗=e}S_{e}=\{s\in S\mid s^{*}s=ss^{*}=e\}is the maximal subgroup at a non-zero idempotente∈E×e\in E^{\times}.

We also extend this to the more general setting of locally finite weak semilattices in §4.1, following the same proof strategy. As with discretisation in K-theory, discretisation in homology admits a dynamical reformulation.

## Theorem E′(Discretisation in homology, a dynamical perspective).

LetG=S⋉XG=S\ltimes Xbe an ample groupoid which admits anSS-invariant regular basis𝒰\mathcal{U}of compact open sets inXX. ThenH∗⁡(G)≅H∗⁡(S⋉𝒰)≅⨁[U]∈S\𝒰H∗⁡(SU),\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(G)\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\mathcal{U})\cong\bigoplus_{[U]\in S\backslash\mathcal{U}}\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S_{U}),

whereSU=(S⋉𝒰)UUS_{U}=(S\ltimes\mathcal{U})^{U}_{U}is the isotropy group atU∈𝒰U\in\mathcal{U}.

The independent resolutions we construct for higher rank graphs and for self-similar groups (see Examples3.16,6.9and6.10) also provide an alternative perspective on the groupoid homology computations undertaken in[20]and[52,51]respectively.

One motivation for studying groupoid homology is that it leads to applications to group homology of topological full groups via[37]. Stein’s groups[65]are generalizations of Thompson groups and Higman–Thompson groups, where we are allowed to vary the conditions on slopes and breakpoints as well as the length of the underlying interval. Stein’s groups were realised as the topological full groups𝑭​(GΠ,A)\bm{F}(G_{\Pi,A})of ample groupoidsGΠ,AG_{\Pi,A}by Tanner[68](see also[69]), based on ideas in[33], and groupoid homology and K-theory computations have been carried out in particular cases in[68,33]. The groupoid perspective was used by Matui to classify Stein’s groups in[43]. Interestingly, in this case the groupoidsGΠ,AG_{\Pi,A}fit into an independent co-resolution of length11, in the sense that they can be described as complements of the formG0∖G−1G_{0}\setminus G_{-1}, whereG0G_{0}andG−1G_{-1}are independent ample groupoids, andG−1G_{-1}is the restriction ofG0G_{0}to a closed invariant subset ofG0(0)G_{0}^{(0)}. This, combined with discretisation, leads to concrete spectral sequences computing groupoid homology and K-theory for underlying groupoids of Stein’s groups. In particular, we obtain the following concrete, sufficient criteria for Stein’s groups to be rationally acyclic.
The parameters(Π,A)(\Pi,A)for the underlying groupoidsGΠ,AG_{\Pi,A}of Stein’s groups are a multiplicative subgroupΠ\Piofℝ>0\mathbb{R}_{>0}and a subgroupA⊆(ℝ,+)A\subseteq(\mathbb{R},+)such thatΠ\Piacts on the monoidA+:=A∩ℝ≥0A_{+}:=A\cap\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}by multiplication.

## Corollary E(see Corollary6.13).

If one of the generators ofΠ\Pican be chosen as a number inℚ>0∖{1}⊆ℝ>0∖{1}\mathbb{Q}_{>0}\setminus\left\{1\right\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{>0}\setminus\left\{1\right\}, thenHn⁡(GΠ,A)⊗ℚ=0\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{n}(G_{\Pi,A})\otimes\mathbb{Q}=0for allnn, and thus the corresponding Stein’s group𝑭​(GΠ,A)\bm{F}(G_{\Pi,A})is rationally acyclic.

We also obtain sufficient criteria for Stein’s groups to be integrally acyclic.

Let us now present the main ideas behind the proofs of discretisation and independent resolutions.

The proof strategy for TheoremAis similar to the one developed in[50], which is based on the ABC spectral sequence and its functoriality with respect to groupoid correspondences. In[50], a spectral sequence argument then reduces TheoremAto discretisation in groupoid homology (this reduction uses the torsion-free isotropy assumption), which is then established by analysing chain complexes. In general, i.e., without the torsion-free isotropy assumption, we have to work with spectral sequences involving more complicated invariants, and the challenge we overcome in this paper is to establish the desired isomorphisms at the level of these more sophisticated analogues of groupoid homology.

In the context of TheoremB, the main novelty of our paper is the development of a systematic way of producing systems of finite covers which lead to independent resolutions recursively following a concrete algorithm. At the technical level, the core exactness result is Theorem3.13, which makes sure that our algorithm does indeed produce an independent resolution, and which is the key improvement compared to previous results in[38,39], where such an exactness result was only established under strong assumptions. We have also developed a systematic approach to finding the data needed to construct an independent resolution, in the language of equivariant representations of semilattices and more generally locally finite weak semilattices. In particular, the notion of a thorough system of finite covers (Definition3.7) provides a practical way to check that a system of finite covers produces a resolution of the desired groupoid (see Lemma3.9).

The present paper is structured as follows: In Section2, we recall the notion of locally finite weak semilattices from[47, § 8], study an equivariant version and develop a new, alternative perspective in terms of topological dynamical systems. Independent resolutions are discussed in Section3, where we also develop our general construction based on systems of finite covers. Again, we work out an interpretation from the point of view of topological dynamics. Throughout Sections2and3, we use the concrete example of higher rank graphs and their groupoids to illustrate our ideas. Section4treats the crucial idea of discretisation. First, we establish discretisation in groupoid homology in Section4.1. Secondly, we formulate discretisation in K-theory in Section4.2. Since the proof of K-theoretic discretisation is long and technical, it occupies a separate section (Section5). Finally, applications and examples are presented in Section6. In AppendixA, we explain how groupoid correspondences lead toC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebraic correspondences in an equivariant setting. This is needed in Section5.

## 2.Inverse semigroup actions on locally finite weak semilattices

In this section, we recall the notion of locally finite weak semilattices (following[47, § 8]) and explain how to constructC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras from these structures. We then develop an alternative, dual picture, which emphasizes the topological perspective. Moreover, we consider the equivariant setting by adding an inverse semigroup action.

First we fix notation and conventions for inverse semigroups and étale groupoids. An étale groupoidGGis a topological groupoid whose range and source maps are local homeomorphisms; in this article the unit spaceG0G^{0}is assumed to be locally compact and Hausdorff, butGGmay be non-Hausdorff. Inverse semigroups and semilattices are assumed to have a0element, and we denote the semilattice of idempotents in an inverse semigroupSSbyE​(S)E(S). There is a natural partial order≤\leqonSSgiven bys≤ts\leq tifs∈t​E​(S)s\in tE(S), or equivalentlys∈E​(S)​ts\in E(S)t. Inverse semigroupsSSact on setsXXby partial bijections; eachs∈Ss\in Sacts as a bijection writtenx↦s.xx\mapsto s.xfrom its domaindomX⁡(s)⊆X\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s)\subseteq Xto its rangeranx⁡(s)⊆X\operatorname{ran}_{x}(s)\subseteq X, anddomX⁡(0)=ranX⁡(0)=∅\operatorname{dom}_{X}(0)=\operatorname{ran}_{X}(0)=\emptyset. An action ofSSon a locally compact Hausdorff spaceXXis by partial homeomorphisms; in this casedomX⁡(s)\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s)andranX⁡(s)\operatorname{ran}_{X}(s)are assumed open ands:domX⁡(s)→ranX⁡(s)s\colon\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s)\to\operatorname{ran}_{X}(s)a homeomorphism. The transformation groupoidS⋉XS\ltimes Xis an étale groupoid with unit spaceXXgiven byS⋉X:-{(s,x)∈S×X|x∈domX(s)}/∼,S\ltimes X\coloneq\left\{(s,x)\in S\times X\;|\;x\in\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s)\right\}/{\sim},

where(s1,x1)∼(s2,x2)(s_{1},x_{1})\sim(s_{2},x_{2})if and only ifx1=x2x_{1}=x_{2}and there existss≤s1,s2s\leq s_{1},s_{2}withx1=x2∈domX⁡(s)x_{1}=x_{2}\in\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s). ## 2.1.Locally finite weak semilattices

## Definition 2.1.

Aweak semilatticeLLis a poset with a zero element0(a necessarily unique element with the property that0≤l0\leq lfor alll∈Ll\in L) such that for alll1,l2∈Ll_{1},l_{2}\in Lthere exists a finite subsetF⊆{l∈L|l≤l1,l≤l2}F\subseteq\left\{l\in L\;|\;l\leq l_{1},\,l\leq l_{2}\right\}such that for alll∈Ll\in Lwithl≤l1l\leq l_{1},l≤l2l\leq l_{2}, there existsf∈Ff\in Fwithl≤fl\leq f. Definel1↓l2l_{1}\mathop{\downarrow}l_{2}as the set of maximal elements in{l∈L|l≤l1,l≤l2}\left\{l\in L\;|\;l\leq l_{1},\,l\leq l_{2}\right\}.

A subsetF¯\bar{F}of a weak semilattice is called↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed if for allx,y∈F¯x,y\in\bar{F},x↓y⊆F¯x\mathop{\downarrow}y\subseteq\bar{F}. We call a weak semilatticeLLlocally finiteif for any finite subsetF⊆LF\subseteq Lthere exists a finite subsetF¯⊆L\bar{F}\subseteq Lwhich is↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed such thatF⊆F¯F\subseteq\bar{F}.

Note that in a weak semilatticeLL,l1↓l2l_{1}\mathop{\downarrow}l_{2}is always a finite set for alll1,l2∈Ll_{1},l_{2}\in L.

In the following, we will use the following notation: Given a posetLLwith zero element0, and a subsetF⊆LF\subseteq L, we writeF×:-F∖{0}F^{\times}\coloneq F\setminus\left\{0\right\}. A subset ofLLis called a down-set if wheneverllis in the subset andk∈Lk\in Lsatisfiesk≤lk\leq l, thenkkhas to be in the subset. Moreover, forxxandyyinLL, we writex⟂yx\perp yif the only element belowxxandyyis0. In that case, we say thatxxandyyare disjoint or orthogonal. This happens in a weak semilattice if and only ifx↓y={0}x\mathop{\downarrow}y=\{0\}. Given a finite subsetFFof a weak semilatticeLL, we write↓f∈Ff\mathop{\big\downarrow}_{f\in F}\,ffor the set of maximal elements in{k∈L|k≤f​for all​f∈F}\left\{k\in L\;|\;k\leq f\text{ for all }f\in F\right\}.

## Remark 2.2.

A semilatticeEEis always a locally finite weak semilattice because for anye,f∈Ee,f\in Ewe havee↓f={e​f}e\mathop{\downarrow}f=\left\{ef\right\}, and for any finite subsetF⊆EF\subseteq Ethe setF¯\bar{F}of finite products of elements inFFis↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed.

In §2and §3, we will illustrate our concepts and results using higher rank graphs as examples, which we will develop step by step.

## Example 2.3.

Letℤ≥0={0,1,2,…}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}=\left\{0,1,2,\dotsc\right\}be the additive monoid of non-negative integers, and letkkbe an integer withk≥1k\geq 1. Ahigher rank graphΛ\Lambdaof rankkk(orkk-graph) is a small category with aℤ≥0k\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}-valued degree map, i.e., a functord:Λ→ℤ≥0kd\colon\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}such that the followingunique factorisation propertyholds: For allν∈Λ\nu\in\Lambdawithd​(ν)=p​qd(\nu)=pq, there exist unique elementsλ,μ∈Λ\lambda,\mu\in\Lambdawithν=λ​μ\nu=\lambda\mu,d​(λ)=pd(\lambda)=pandd​(μ)=qd(\mu)=q.

C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras attached to higher rank graphs have been introduced in[31](see also[59,60]).

SetLΛ:-Λ∪{0}L_{\Lambda}\coloneq\Lambda\cup\{0\}, ordered byλ≤μ\lambda\leq\muifλ​Λ⊆μ​Λ\lambda\Lambda\subseteq\mu\Lambda, i.e. there isλ′∈Λ\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambdawithλ​λ′=μ\lambda\lambda^{\prime}=\mu.111This has the slightly awkward consequence that the degree mapddreverses inequalities, i.e.λ≤μ\lambda\leq\muimpliesd​(λ)≥d​(μ)d(\lambda)\geq d(\mu).The higher rank graphΛ\Lambdais called finitely aligned ifLΛL_{\Lambda}is a weak semilattice. In this case it is automatically locally finite; let us explain why. Forλ,μ∈Λ\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda, the setλ↓μ\lambda\mathop{\downarrow}\muconsists of minimal common extensions ofλ\lambdaandμ\mu, which are all orthogonal. That is, for distinctν,ξ∈λ↓μ\nu,\xi\in\lambda\mathop{\downarrow}\mu, we haveν⟂ξ\nu\perp\xi. Minimal common extensionsν\nuandξ\xiofλ\lambdaandμ\mumust satisfyd​(ν)=d​(ξ)=d​(λ)∨d​(μ)d(\nu)=d(\xi)=d(\lambda)\vee d(\mu)(where∨\veedenotes the least common upper bound in the monoidℤ≥0k\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}), and ifν\nuandξ\xihave a common extension thenν=ξ\nu=\xiby the unique factorisation property. Now, given a finite setF⊆ΛF\subseteq\Lambda, consider the finite setF¯=⋃A⊆F↓f∈Af.\bar{F}=\bigcup_{A\subseteq F}\mathop{\big\downarrow}_{f\in A}\,f.

This containsFFand is↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed because, givenλ∈↓f∈Af\lambda\in\mathop{\big\downarrow}_{f\in A}\,fandλ′∈↓f′∈A′f′\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathop{\big\downarrow}_{f^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}}\,f^{\prime}, we haveλ↓λ′⊆↓g∈A∪A′g\lambda\mathop{\downarrow}\lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\mathop{\big\downarrow}_{g\in A\cup A^{\prime}}\,g.

Given akk-graphΛ\Lambdaand an elementλ∈Λ\lambda\in\Lambda, let𝔡​(λ)\mathfrak{d}(\lambda)denote the domain ofλ\lambdaand𝔱​(λ)\mathfrak{t}(\lambda)the codomain ofλ\lambda. Given an objectvvinΛ\Lambda, note thatv​Λ=𝔱−1​(v)v\Lambda=\mathfrak{t}^{-1}(v). Moreover, letei∈ℤ≥0ke_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}be theii-th generator (for1≤i≤k1\leq i\leq k) and defineΛ(i):=d−1​(ei).\Lambda^{(i)}:=d^{-1}(e_{i}).

Thekk-graphΛ\Lambdaisrow-finiteif for every objectvvinΛ\Lambdaand1≤i≤k1\leq i\leq k, we have#​v​Λ(i)<∞\#\,v\Lambda^{(i)}<\infty. In this case,Λ\Lambdais finitely aligned. It issource-freeif0<#​v​Λ(i)0<\#\,v\Lambda^{(i)}for eachii.

Next, let us define theC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebra of a locally finite weak semilattice. We need the following notation. LetDDbe a commutative algebra andFFa finite set of idempotents inDD. Define⋁p∈Fp:-∑∅≠F′⊆F(−1)#​F′−1​∏p∈F′p.\bigvee_{p\in F}p\coloneq\sum_{\emptyset\neq F^{\prime}\subseteq F}(-1)^{\#\,F^{\prime}-1}\prod_{p\in F^{\prime}}p.

## Definition 2.4.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice. TheC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebraC∗⁡(L)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)ofLLis the universalC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebra which is commutative and generated by projections{pl|l∈L}\left\{p_{l}\;|\;l\in L\right\}subject to the relationsp0=0p_{0}=0andpk​pl=⋁m∈k↓lpmfor all​k,l∈L.p_{k}p_{l}=\bigvee_{m\in k\mathop{\downarrow}l}p_{m}\quad\quad\quad\text{ for all }k,l\in L.

To see thatC∗⁡(L)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)is non-trivial, let us construct a concrete representation. Consider the Hilbert spaceℓ2​(L×)\ell^{2}(L^{\times})with canonical orthonormal basis{δx|x∈L×}\left\{\delta_{x}\;|\;x\in L^{\times}\right\}. Givenl∈Ll\in L, letPlP_{l}be the orthogonal projection onto the closed subspacespan¯​({δk|k≤l})\overline{{\rm span}}(\left\{\delta_{k}\;|\;k\leq l\right\}). LetCr∗⁡(L):-C∗⁡({Pl|l∈L})⊆ℒ​(ℓ2​(L×))\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(L)\coloneq\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\left\{P_{l}\;|\;l\in L\right\})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\ell^{2}(L^{\times})). Clearly,P0=0P_{0}=0andPk​Pl=⋁m∈k↓lPmP_{k}P_{l}=\bigvee_{m\in k\mathop{\downarrow}l}P_{m}for allk,l∈Lk,l\in L. Hence, by universal property ofC∗⁡(L)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L), there exists a homomorphismπr:C∗⁡(L)→Cr∗⁡(L)\pi_{r}\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(L)sendingplp_{l}toPlP_{l}for alll∈Ll\in L.

Let us now studyC∗⁡(L)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)in more detail.

## Lemma 2.5.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice andF¯\bar{F}a↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed finite subset ofLL. For every subsetF⊆F¯F\subseteq\bar{F}, we have⋁k∈Fpk∈ℤ​-​span​({pl|l∈F¯})\bigvee_{k\in F}\,p_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{-}{\rm span}(\left\{p_{l}\;|\;l\in\bar{F}\right\}).

## Proof.

Givenl∈F¯l\in\bar{F}, defineℓ​(l):-#​({k∈F¯×|k≤l})\ell(l)\coloneq\#(\left\{k\in\bar{F}^{\times}\;|\;k\leq l\right\}), and setℓ​(F):-max⁡{ℓ​(l)|l∈F}\ell(F)\coloneq\max\left\{\ell(l)\;|\;l\in F\right\}. We proceed by induction onmin⁡(#​F,ℓ​(F))\min(\#\,F,\ell(F)). Ifℓ​(F)=1\ell(F)=1, then allpkp_{k}(fork∈Fk\in F) are pairwise disjoint, so that⋁k∈Fpk=∑k∈Fpk\bigvee_{k\in F}\,p_{k}=\sum_{k\in F}p_{k}. If#​F=1\#\,F=1, then there is nothing to prove. For the induction step, writeF={k}∪F′F=\left\{k\right\}\cup F^{\prime}. Thenq:-⋁k′∈F′pk′q\coloneq\bigvee_{k^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}}p_{k^{\prime}}lies inℤ​-​span​({pl|l∈F¯})\mathbb{Z}\text{-}{\rm span}(\left\{p_{l}\;|\;l\in\bar{F}\right\}). Moreover,⋁l∈Fpl=pk∨q=pk+q−pk​q=pk+q−⋁k′∈F′pk​pk′=pk+q−⋁k′∈F′⋁j∈k↓k′pj.\bigvee_{l\in F}p_{l}=p_{k}\vee q=p_{k}+q-p_{k}q=p_{k}+q-\bigvee_{k^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}}p_{k}p_{k^{\prime}}=p_{k}+q-\bigvee_{k^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}}\bigvee_{j\in k\mathop{\downarrow}k^{\prime}}\,p_{j}.

As everyj∈⋃k′∈F′k↓k′j\in\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}}k\mathop{\downarrow}k^{\prime}satisfiesj≤k′j\leq k^{\prime}for somek′∈F′k^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}, we haveℓ​(⋃k′∈F′k↓k′)≤ℓ​(F′)\ell(\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}}k\mathop{\downarrow}k^{\prime})\leq\ell(F^{\prime}). It hence follows that⋁k′∈F′⋁j∈k↓k′pj∈ℤ​-​span​({pl|l∈F¯})\bigvee_{k^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}}\bigvee_{j\in k\mathop{\downarrow}k^{\prime}}\,p_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{-}{\rm span}(\left\{p_{l}\;|\;l\in\bar{F}\right\})by induction hypothesis.
∎

## Lemma 2.6.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice.
- (i)

IfF⊆LF\subseteq Lis a finite↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed subset, thenC∗({pf|f∈F})=span(({pf|f∈F})\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\left\{p_{f}\;|\;f\in F\right\})={\rm span}((\left\{p_{f}\;|\;f\in F\right\})is finite dimensional.
- (ii)

We haveC∗⁡(L)≅lim→F⁡C∗⁡({pf|f∈F})\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)\cong\varinjlim_{F}\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\left\{p_{f}\;|\;f\in F\right\}), where the inductive limit is taken over all finite↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed subsetsFFofLL, ordered by inclusion.
- (iii)

πr:C∗⁡(L)→Cr∗⁡(L)\pi_{r}\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(L)is an isomorphism.
- (iv)

The set{pl|l∈L×}\left\{p_{l}\;|\;l\in L^{\times}\right\}is linearly independent (inC∗⁡(L)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)).
- (v)

LetDDbe a commutativeC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebra generated by projections{ql|l∈L}\left\{q_{l}\;|\;l\in L\right\}such thatq0=0q_{0}=0andqk​ql=⋁m∈k↓lqmq_{k}q_{l}=\bigvee_{m\in k\mathop{\downarrow}l}q_{m}for allk,l∈Lk,l\in L. Then the canonical homomorphismπ:C∗⁡(L)→D\pi\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)\to Dsendingplp_{l}toqlq_{l}is an isomorphism if and only if the set{ql|l∈L×}\left\{q_{l}\;|\;l\in L^{\times}\right\}is linearly independent (inDD).

## Proof.

(i) follows from Lemma2.5. (ii) is clear. For (iii), take a finite↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed subsetF⊆LF\subseteq L. For everyl∈F×l\in F^{\times}, consider the projectionPl′:-Pl−⋁k∈F,k⪇lPk∈C∗⁡({Pf|f∈F×}).P^{\prime}_{l}\coloneq P_{l}-\bigvee_{k\in F,\,k\lneq l}P_{k}\in\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\left\{P_{f}\;|\;f\in F^{\times}\right\}).

The set{Pl′|l∈F×}\left\{P^{\prime}_{l}\;|\;l\in F^{\times}\right\}is linearly independent becausePl′​(δm)=1P^{\prime}_{l}(\delta_{m})=1ifl=ml=mandPl′​(δm)=0P^{\prime}_{l}(\delta_{m})=0ifl≠ml\neq m. Hence we have#​F×\displaystyle\#\,F^{\times}≤dim​(C∗⁡({Pf|f∈F×}))\displaystyle\leq{\rm dim}\,(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\left\{P_{f}\;|\;f\in F^{\times}\right\}))≤dim​(C∗⁡({pf|f∈F×}))=dim​(span​({Pf|f∈F×}))≤#​F×.\displaystyle\leq{\rm dim}\,(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\left\{p_{f}\;|\;f\in F^{\times}\right\}))={\rm dim}\,({\rm span}(\left\{P_{f}\;|\;f\in F^{\times}\right\}))\leq\#\,F^{\times}.

It follows that the restriction ofπr\pi_{r}toC∗⁡({pf|f∈F×})\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\left\{p_{f}\;|\;f\in F^{\times}\right\})is isometric, and thusπr\pi_{r}itself is isometric by (ii). This also shows that{Pl|l∈L×}\left\{P_{l}\;|\;l\in L^{\times}\right\}is linearly independent (inCr∗⁡(L)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(L)), which implies (iv) because of (iii). Finally, (v) follows by a similar argument: If{ql|l∈L×}\left\{q_{l}\;|\;l\in L^{\times}\right\}is linearly independent (inDD), thendim(C∗({qf|f∈F×})=#F×{\rm dim}\,(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\left\{q_{f}\;|\;f\in F^{\times}\right\})=\#\,F^{\times}for every finite↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed subsetFFofLL, so that the restriction ofπ\pitoC∗⁡({pf|f∈F×})\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\left\{p_{f}\;|\;f\in F^{\times}\right\})is isometric. Again (ii) implies thatπ\piitself is isometric.

In the following, we develop a topological perspective.

## Definition 2.7.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice. AcharacteronLLis a mapχ:L→{0,1}\chi:\>L\to\left\{0,1\right\}with the property thatχ​(0)=0\chi(0)=0,χ\chiis non-zero (i.e., there existsl∈Ll\in Lsuch thatχ​(l)≠0\chi(l)\neq 0), and, for alll1,l2∈Ll_{1},l_{2}\in L, we have thatχ​(l1)=1\chi(l_{1})=1andχ​(l2)=1\chi(l_{2})=1holds if and only if there existsl∈l1↓l2l\in l_{1}\mathop{\downarrow}l_{2}withχ​(l)=1\chi(l)=1. LetL^\widehat{L}be the set of all characters onLL. We equipL^\widehat{L}with the topology of pointwise convergence. We introduce the notationL^​(l):-{χ∈L^|χ​(l)=1}\widehat{L}(l)\coloneq\left\{\chi\in\widehat{L}\;|\;\chi(l)=1\right\}forl∈L×l\in L^{\times}. For each non-zero elementl∈L×l\in L^{\times}, theprincipal character atllis the characterχl\chi_{l}defined byχl−1​(1)={k∈L∣k≥l}\chi_{l}^{-1}(1)=\{k\in L\mid k\geq l\}.

The following are immediate consequences of the definitions and the universal property ofC∗⁡(L)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L).

## Lemma 2.8.

We have a homeomorphismL^→∼Spec​(C∗⁡(L))\widehat{L}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}{\rm Spec\,}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L))sending a characterχ∈L^\chi\in\widehat{L}to the elementC∗⁡(L)→ℂ,pl↦χ​(l)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)\to\mathbb{C},\,p_{l}\mapsto\chi(l)inSpec​(C∗⁡(L)){\rm Spec\,}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)). The inverse sends the elementω:C∗⁡(L)→ℂ\omega\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)\to\mathbb{C}inSpec​(C∗⁡(L)){\rm Spec\,}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L))to the characterχ:L→{0,1},l↦ω​(pl)\chi\colon L\to\left\{0,1\right\},\,l\mapsto\omega(p_{l}).

## Corollary 2.9.

We have an isomorphismC∗⁡(L)≅C0​(L^)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)\cong C_{0}(\widehat{L})sendingplp_{l}to the characteristic function1L^​(l)1_{\widehat{L}(l)}.

As a next step, let us explain how our setup can be viewed as a generalisation of the setup considered in[15, § 2], with locally finite weak semilattices replacing semilattices. Consider𝒱:-{L^​(l)∣l∈L×}∪{∅}\mathcal{V}\coloneq\{\widehat{L}(l)\mid l\in L^{\times}\}\cup\left\{\emptyset\right\}. Then𝒱\mathcal{V}is a set of compact open subsets ofL^\widehat{L}which is a poset with respect to⊆\subseteq, and the mapL→𝒱L\to\mathcal{V}sendingl∈L×l\in L^{\times}toL^​(l)\widehat{L}(l)and0to∅\emptysetis a poset isomorphism. In particular, it follows that𝒱\mathcal{V}is a locally finite weak semilattice. However,𝒱\mathcal{V}has the special property thatV∩W=⋃U∈V↓WUV\cap W=\bigcup_{U\in V\mathop{\downarrow}W}Ufor allV,W∈𝒱V,W\in\mathcal{V}. Moreover,𝒱\mathcal{V}has two additional properties; it generates the set of all compact open subsets ofL^\widehat{L}under finite unions and relative complements, and it satisfies the independence condition: givenV,V1,…,Vi∈𝒱×V,V_{1},\dotsc,V_{i}\in\mathcal{V}^{\times}withV⊆⋃h=1iVhV\subseteq\bigcup_{h=1}^{i}V_{h}, then there exists1≤h≤i1\leq h\leq isuch thatV⊆VhV\subseteq V_{h}.

This motivates the following definition.

## Definition 2.10.

ArepresentationUUof a weak semilatticeLLon a totally disconnected locally compact Hausdorff spaceXXis an order-preserving functionU:L→𝒪c​(X)U\colon L\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)withU​(0)=∅U(0)=\emptysetandU​(l1)∩U​(l2)=⋃l∈l1↓l2U​(l)U(l_{1})\cap U(l_{2})=\bigcup_{l\in l_{1}\mathop{\downarrow}l_{2}}U(l)for anyl1,l2∈Ll_{1},l_{2}\in L. The representationUUisgeneratingif
- (GEN)

{U​(l)∣l∈L}\{U(l)\mid l\in L\}generates𝒪c​(X)\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)under finite unions and relative complementsV∖WV\setminus WforV⊆WV\subseteq W,

andindependentif
- (IND)

wheneverl,l1,…,ln∈Ll,l_{1},\dots,l_{n}\in LsatisfyU​(l)⊆⋃i=1nU​(li)U(l)\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U(l_{i}), there is1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq nsuch thatl=lil=l_{i}.

Independence implies that the representation is faithful in the sense thatUUis injective. A faithful representation given by a subsetL⊆𝒪c​(X)L\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)will also be called a weak semilatticeinXX.

## Lemma 2.11.

LetU:L→𝒪c​(X)U\colon L\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)be a generating representation of a locally finite weak semilatticeLLonXX. Then there is a homeomorphismX→\displaystyle X\toL^U:={χ∈L^|for all​f,l1,…,ln∈L​with​U​(f)⊆⋃iU​(li),χ​(f)=1​implies​χ​(li)=1​for some​i},\displaystyle\widehat{L}_{U}:=\left\{\chi\in\widehat{L}\;|\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{for all }f,l_{1},\dotsc,l_{n}\in L\text{ with }U(f)\subseteq\bigcup_{i}U(l_{i}),\\
\chi(f)=1\text{ implies }\chi(l_{i})=1\text{ for some }i\end{subarray}\right\},x↦\displaystyle x\mapstoχx,\displaystyle\chi_{x},

fromXXto the closed subsetL^U⊆L^\widehat{L}_{U}\subseteq\widehat{L}, sendingx∈Xx\in Xto the characterχx\chi_{x}determined byχx​(l)=1\chi_{x}(l)=1if and only ifx∈U​(l)x\in U(l). The inverse map sends a characterχ∈L^U\chi\in\widehat{L}_{U}to the unique pointx∈Xx\in Xsuch that{x}=(⋂l∈L,χ​(l)=1U​(l))∖(⋃k∈L,χ​(k)=0U​(k)).\left\{x\right\}=\Big(\bigcap_{l\in L,\,\chi(l)=1}U(l)\Big)\setminus\Big(\bigcup_{k\in L,\,\chi(k)=0}U(k)\Big).

This homeomorphism sendsU​(l)U(l)to{χ∈L^U|χ​(l)=1}\left\{\chi\in\widehat{L}_{U}\;|\;\chi(l)=1\right\}.
IfUUis moreover independent, thenL^U=L^\widehat{L}_{U}=\widehat{L}and this determines a homeomorphismX≅L^X\cong\widehat{L}.

## Proof.

We first note that ifUUis independent it is clear thatL^U=L^\widehat{L}_{U}=\widehat{L}.

We must check that givenχ∈L^U\chi\in\widehat{L}_{U}, the set(⋂l∈L,χ​(l)=1U​(l))∖(⋃k∈L,χ​(k)=0U​(k))\Big(\bigcap_{l\in L,\,\chi(l)=1}U(l)\Big)\setminus\Big(\bigcup_{k\in L,\,\chi(k)=0}U(k)\Big)is a singleton.

This is the intersection of the compact setsU​(l)∖U​(k)U(l)\setminus U(k)fork,l∈Lk,l\in Lwithχ​(k)=0\chi(k)=0andχ​(l)=1\chi(l)=1. We will argue this is nonempty by the finite intersection property, so consider(k1,l1),…,(kn,ln)(k_{1},l_{1}),\dots,(k_{n},l_{n})as above. Then there existsl∈↓i=1nlil\in\mathop{\big\downarrow}_{i=1}^{n}l_{i}withχ​(l)=1\chi(l)=1, and so⋂i=1nU​(li)∖⋃i=1nU​(ki)⊇U​(l)∖⋃i=1nU​(ki)\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}U(l_{i})\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U(k_{i})\supseteq U(l)\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U(k_{i})is nonempty becauseχ∈L^U\chi\in\widehat{L}_{U}.

Suppose now that it contains distinct elementsx≠yx\neq y. BecauseUUis generating, there isl∈Ll\in Lsuch thatU​(l)U(l)either containsxxbut notyyoryybut notxx. Without loss of generality we havex∈U​(l)x\in U(l), which implies thatχ​(l)=1\chi(l)=1. But this in turn impliesy∈U​(l)y\in U(l), a contradiction.

It is clear that this determines an inverse to the continuous mapx↦χxx\mapsto\chi_{x}.
Ifx∈U​(l)x\in U(l), thenχx​(l)=1\chi_{x}(l)=1and conversely ifχ=χx\chi=\chi_{x}satisfiesχ​(l)=1\chi(l)=1, thenx∈U​(l)x\in U(l). Thus the compact open setU​(l)U(l)is sent to the compact open set{χ∈L^U∣χ​(l)=1}\{\chi\in\widehat{L}_{U}\mid\chi(l)=1\}. BecauseUUis generating andXXis totally disconnected, we conclude thatx↦χxx\mapsto\chi_{x}is open and thus a homeomorphism.
∎

## Remark 2.12.

WhenLLis a semilattice, an independent generating representation ofLLonXXis precisely the data of a regular basis in the sense of[15, § 2]. A general locally finite weak semilatticeLLwith an independent generating representationUUonXXis a semilattice if and only if{U​(l)∣l∈L×}\{U(l)\mid l\in L^{\times}\}is a regular basis. Moreover,[15, Proposition 2.12]tells us that ifXXis a totally disconnected, locally compact Hausdorff space which is second countable, then there always exists a regular basis, and hence an independent generating representation of a semilattice onXX.

Later on, we will need the following definition.

## Definition 2.13.

Given a locally finite weak semilatticeLL, letℤ​L\mathbb{Z}Lbe the free abelian group generated byL×L^{\times}, i.e.,ℤ​L=⨁l∈L×ℤ\mathbb{Z}L=\bigoplus_{l\in L^{\times}}\mathbb{Z}. We write elements ofℤ​L\mathbb{Z}Las finite sums of the form∑l∈L×al​l\sum_{l\in L^{\times}}a_{l}\,l, foral∈ℤa_{l}\in\mathbb{Z}.

The following is an immediate consequence of Lemma2.6.

## Corollary 2.14.

We have an isomorphism of abelian groupsℤ​L→∼ℤ​-​span​({pl|l∈L}),l↦pl.\mathbb{Z}L\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\mathbb{Z}\text{-}{\rm span}(\left\{p_{l}\;|\;l\in L\right\}),\,l\mapsto p_{l}.

Moreover, Lemma2.5implies thatℤ​-​span​({pl|l∈L})\mathbb{Z}\text{-}{\rm span}(\left\{p_{l}\;|\;l\in L\right\})is aℤ\mathbb{Z}-algebra, where the multiplication is induced by the one inC∗⁡(L)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L). Therefore, this enables us to equipℤ​L\mathbb{Z}Lwith aℤ\mathbb{Z}-algebra structure such that the isomorphism provided by Corollary2.14becomes an isomorphism ofℤ\mathbb{Z}-algebras. In particular, we can introduce the following.

## Definition 2.15.

Given a finite subsetF⊆LF\subseteq L, define⋁f∈Ff∈ℤ​L\bigvee_{f\in F}\,f\in\mathbb{Z}Las the element ofℤ​L\mathbb{Z}Lthat is mapped to⋁f∈Fpf∈ℤ​-​span​({pl|l∈L})\bigvee_{f\in F}\,p_{f}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{-}{\rm span}(\left\{p_{l}\;|\;l\in L\right\})under the isomorphism from Corollary2.14.

Our topological perspective gives another picture forℤ​L\mathbb{Z}L. In the following,Cc​(L^,ℤ)C_{c}(\widehat{L},\mathbb{Z})denotes theℤ\mathbb{Z}-algebra of compactly supported, continuous functionsL^→ℤ\widehat{L}\to\mathbb{Z}. Sinceℤ\mathbb{Z}is viewed as a discrete space, a functionL^→ℤ\widehat{L}\to\mathbb{Z}is continuous if and only if it is locally constant.

## Lemma 2.16.

There is an isomorphism ofℤ\mathbb{Z}-algebrasℤ​L→∼Cc​(L^,ℤ),l↦1L^​(l).\mathbb{Z}L\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}C_{c}(\widehat{L},\mathbb{Z}),l\mapsto 1_{\widehat{L}(l)}.

## Proof.

Our map is given by the compositionℤ​L→∼ℤ​-​span​({pl|l∈L})↪C∗⁡(L)→∼C0​(L^),\mathbb{Z}L\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\mathbb{Z}\text{-}{\rm span}(\left\{p_{l}\;|\;l\in L\right\})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(L)\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}C_{0}(\widehat{L}),

where the first arrow is provided by Corollary2.14, the second arrow is the canonical inclusion, and the third arrow is the isomorphism from Corollary2.9. It hence suffices to show that the image of our map is all ofCc​(L^,ℤ)C_{c}(\widehat{L},\mathbb{Z}). This follows from the observation thatCc​(L^,ℤ)C_{c}(\widehat{L},\mathbb{Z})is generated as an abelian group by characteristic functions1V1_{V}of basic compact open sets of the formV=L^​(l)∖(⋃k∈FL^​(k))V=\widehat{L}(l)\setminus(\bigcup_{k\in F}\widehat{L}(k)), whereFFis a finite subset of{k∈L|k≤l}\left\{k\in L\;|\;k\leq l\right\}, and such elements1V1_{V}are in the image of our mapℤ​L→Cc​(L^,ℤ)\mathbb{Z}L\to C_{c}(\widehat{L},\mathbb{Z}).
∎

## 2.2.The equivariant setting

The most canonical action of an inverse semigroupSSon a locally finite weak semilattice is the actionS↷ES\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Eon the semilatticeEEof idempotents inSS. The action is given ats∈Ss\in SbydomE⁡(s)={e∈E|e≤s−1​s}\operatorname{dom}_{E}(s)=\left\{e\in E\;|\;e\leq s^{-1}s\right\}ands.e:-s​e​s−1s.e\coloneq ses^{-1}. In this case,S⋉E^S\ltimes\widehat{E}is Paterson’s universal groupoid ofSS(see[56, § 4.3], but with a small difference arising from the treatment of0∈S0\in Sas pointed out in[16, § 5.5.4]).

Now let us develop a topological perspective in the equivariant setting, following[34, Definition 2.12].

for eachs∈Ss\in Swe havedomX⁡(s)=⋃l∈domL⁡(s)U​(l)\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s)=\bigcup_{l\in\operatorname{dom}_{L}(s)}U(l), and

for eachs∈Ss\in Sandl∈domL⁡(s)l\in\operatorname{dom}_{L}(s), we haves.(U(l))=U(s.l)s.(U(l))=U(s.l).

ranX⁡(s)=⋃l∈ranL⁡(s)U​(l)\operatorname{ran}_{X}(s)=\bigcup_{l\in\operatorname{ran}_{L}(s)}U(l)for everys∈Ss\in S,
- •

U|domL⁡(s):domL⁡(s)→𝒪c​(domX⁡(s))U|_{\operatorname{dom}_{L}(s)}\colon\operatorname{dom}_{L}(s)\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s))is a representation of the locally finite weak semilatticedomL⁡(s)\operatorname{dom}_{L}(s)ondomX⁡(s)\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s), and similarly forU|ranL⁡(s)U|_{\operatorname{ran}_{L}(s)}.

The following is now a straightforward consequence.

## Lemma 2.20.

LetSSbe an inverse semigroup, letSSact on a totally disconnected, locally compact Hausdorff spaceXXand a locally finite weak semilatticeLL, and suppose thatUUis anSS-equivariant generating representation ofLLonXX. IfUUis moreover independent, this yields an isomorphism(S↷X)≅(S↷L^)(S\mathbin{\curvearrowright}X)\cong(S\mathbin{\curvearrowright}\widehat{L}).

## Definition 2.21.

LetS↷XS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Xbe an action of an inverse semigroupSSon a totally disconnected locally compact Hausdorff spaceXX. We sayS↷XS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Xisindependentif there is anSS-equivariant independent generating representation of a locally finite weak semilatticeLLonXX. An ample groupoidGGis calledindependentif there is an independent actionS↷XS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}XwithG≅S⋉XG\cong S\ltimes X.

## 3.Independent resolutions

## 3.1.Definitions and general observations

The idea of independent resolutions is to find “resolutions” of ample groupoids by independent groupoids, i.e. those of the formS⋉L^S\ltimes\widehat{L}as in §2.2.

## Definition 3.1.

LetGGbe an ample groupoid. Anindependent resolutionofGGis given by a sequence of ample groupoidsGiG_{i},i=−1,0,1,2,…i=-1,0,1,2,\dotsc, with the following extra data and subject to the following conditions:
- •

G−1=GG_{-1}=G,
- •

For everyii, a decompositionGi0=Ci∐OiG_{i}^{0}=C_{i}\amalg O_{i}, whereCiC_{i}is a closedGiG_{i}-invariant subspace (and henceOiO_{i}is open and invariant), andC−1=G0C_{-1}=G^{0},
- •

For everyi≥−1i\geq-1, a groupoid isomorphismψi:Gi|Oi→∼Gi+1|Ci+1\psi_{i}\colon G_{i}|_{O_{i}}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}G_{i+1}|_{C_{i+1}}, whereGi|Oi:-{g∈Gi|r​(g),s​(g)∈Oi}G_{i}|_{O_{i}}\coloneq\left\{g\in G_{i}\;|\;r(g),s(g)\in O_{i}\right\}is the restriction ofGiG_{i}toOiO_{i}, and similarly forGi+1|Ci+1G_{i+1}|_{C_{i+1}},
- •

For eachi≥0i\geq 0, an actionSi↷LiS_{i}\mathbin{\curvearrowright}L_{i}of an inverse semigroupSiS_{i}on a locally finite weak semilatticeLiL_{i}with an isomorphismGi≅Si⋉Li^G_{i}\cong S_{i}\ltimes\widehat{L_{i}}.

Note thatψi\psi_{i}restricts to a homeomorphismOi≅Ci+1O_{i}\cong C_{i+1}.

We call(Gi)i(G_{i})_{i}an independent resolution in the strong sense if for eachiiwe haveLi=E​(Si)L_{i}=E(S_{i})(the semilattice of idempotents inSiS_{i}) with the canonical actionSi↷E​(Si)S_{i}\mathbin{\curvearrowright}E(S_{i}).

Let us now explain why independent resolutions always exist (even in the strong sense). First of all, given an ample groupoidGG, we can always find an inverse semigroupSSof compact open bisections inGGsuch thatG≅S⋉XG\cong S\ltimes X(see[32, Proposition 2.2]). Furthermore, we can choose a family𝒰\mathcal{U}of compact open subsets ofXXsuch that𝒰\mathcal{U}generates the topology ofXXin the sense of[15, § 2](see also[14]), i.e.𝒰\mathcal{U}generates the entire family of compact open subsets through finite unions, finite intersections and relative complements. LetEEbe the smallest semilattice of compact open subsets ofXXcontaining𝒰\mathcal{U}which isSS-invariant. By construction,EEis anSS-invariant generating semilattice inXX. Thus by Lemma2.20we have the following homeomorphism, identifyingXXwith a closedSS-invariant subspace ofE^\widehat{E}:X→\displaystyle X\to{χ∈E^|for all​f,e1,…,en∈E​with​f=⋃iei​in​X,χ​(f)=1​implies​χ​(ei)=1​for some​i},\displaystyle\left\{\chi\in\widehat{E}\;|\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{for all }f,e_{1},\dotsc,e_{n}\in E\text{ with }f=\bigcup_{i}e_{i}\text{ in }X,\\
\chi(f)=1\text{ implies }\chi(e_{i})=1\text{ for some }i\end{subarray}\right\},x↦\displaystyle x\mapstoχx,\displaystyle\chi_{x},

whereχx​(e)=1\chi_{x}(e)=1if and only ifx∈ex\in e. Hence, we can setG0:-S⋉E^G_{0}\coloneq S\ltimes\widehat{E}, defineC0C_{0}as the subspace ofE^\widehat{E}homeomorphic toXXunder the map above, andO0:-E^∖C0O_{0}\coloneq\widehat{E}\setminus C_{0}. Now proceed inductively, withG0|O0G_{0}|_{O_{0}}in place ofGG. This produces an independent resolution (even in the strong sense).

## 3.2.Systems of finite covers

The above-mentioned existence statement is quite abstract. Let us now present a more systematic way of producing independent resolutions. SupposeG≅S⋉XG\cong S\ltimes Xas before and supposeUUis anSS-equivariant, generating representation of a locally finite weak semilatticeLLinXX. As before, we may by Lemma2.20identifyXXwith the closedSS-invariant subspaceC−1={χ∈L^|for all​l,f1,…,fn∈L​with​U​(l)=⋃iU​(fi),χ​(l)=1​implies​∃i​with​χ​(fi)=1}C_{-1}=\left\{\chi\in\widehat{L}\;|\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{for all }l,f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\in L\text{ with }U(l)=\bigcup_{i}U(f_{i}),\\
\chi(l)=1\text{ implies }\exists i\text{ with }\chi(f_{i})=1\end{subarray}\right\}

ofL^\widehat{L}. We could as before have arrangedLLto be a semilattice, but the extra flexibility allows us to more easily handle situations naturally endowed with a locally finite weak semilattice.

The inclusionX⊆L^X\subseteq\widehat{L}induces a mapCc​(L^,ℤ)↠Cc​(X,ℤ)C_{c}(\widehat{L},\mathbb{Z})\twoheadrightarrow C_{c}(X,\mathbb{Z}). The kernelIIof this map is generated by the indicator functions on relative complements of the form(3.1)L^​(l)∖⋃f∈FL^​(f),\widehat{L}(l)\setminus\bigcup_{f\in F}\widehat{L}(f),

wherel∈Ll\in LandF⊆LF\subseteq Lis a finite set withl=⋃f∈Ffl=\bigcup_{f\in F}finXX. This satisfies the property that ifk∈Lk\in Lis orthogonal to eachf∈Ff\in F, it is also orthogonal toll. We say thatFFis afinite coverofll. This motivates the following general definition.

## Definition 3.2.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice and letl∈Ll\in L. A finite setF⊆LF\subseteq Lis afinite coverofllif
- •

f≤lf\leq lfor eachf∈Ff\in F, and
- •

ifk∈Lk\in Lwithk⟂fk\perp ffor eachf∈Ff\in F, thenk⟂lk\perp l.

A finite coverFFofllistrivialifl∈Fl\in F. The set of maximal elements in a finite cover is again a finite cover; it is natural to only consider finite covers whose elements are all maximal.

Given a representationU:L→𝒪c​(X)U\colon L\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)ofLLon a totally disconnected locally compact Hausdorff spaceXX, we say a finite coverFFofl∈Ll\in LisconcreteifU​(l)=⋃f∈FU​(f)U(l)=\bigcup_{f\in F}U(f).

## Example 3.3.

LetΛ\Lambdabe a finitely aligned higher rank graph and letLΛ=Λ∪{0}L_{\Lambda}=\Lambda\cup\{0\}be its locally finite weak semilattice (see Examples2.3and2.22). A finite coverEEof a vertexv∈Λ0v\in\Lambda^{0}is precisely a finite exhaustive subset in the sense of[21, Definition 3.6]. Supposev∈Λ0v\in\Lambda^{0}is a vertex and1≤i≤k1\leq i\leq kis an index such that0<#​v​Λ(i)<∞0<\#\,v\Lambda^{(i)}<\infty. Then for eachλ∈v​Λ\lambda\in v\Lambdaan explicit finite cover ofλ\lambdais given byλ​Λ(i).\lambda\Lambda^{(i)}.

This is concrete with respect to the representationU:LΛ→𝒪c​(∂Λ)U\colon L_{\Lambda}\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(\partial\Lambda)given byU​(λ)={z∈∂Λ∣z​(d​(λ))=λ}U(\lambda)=\{z\in\partial\Lambda\mid z(d(\lambda))=\lambda\}because any infinite pathz∈∂Λz\in\partial\Lambdastarting withλ\lambdamust start withλ​ϵ\lambda\epsilonfor someϵ∈Λ(i)\epsilon\in\Lambda^{(i)}.

We introduce the following conventions.

## Definition 3.4.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice and letFFbe a finite cover of an elementl∈Ll\in L. We write(l∣F):-l−⋁f∈Ff∈ℤ​L(l\mid F)\coloneq l-\bigvee_{f\in F}f\in\mathbb{Z}L

for the relative complement ofFFinll(the join⋁\bigveewas introduced in Definition2.15). Given further an elementk≤lk\leq l, the finite cover(3.2)k↓F:=max⁡(⋃f∈Fk↓f)k\mathop{\downarrow}F:={\max}\big(\bigcup_{f\in F}k\mathop{\downarrow}f\big)

is given by the set of maximal elements in⋃f∈Fk↓f\bigcup_{f\in F}k\mathop{\downarrow}f, which is itself a finite cover ofkk. For a finite setℱ\mathcal{F}of finite covers ofll, we set(3.3)(l∣ℱ):=l−⋁f∈F∈ℱf=∏F∈ℱ(l∣F)(l\mid\mathcal{F}):=l-\bigvee_{f\in F\in\mathcal{F}}f=\prod_{F\in\mathcal{F}}(l\mid F)

We writek↓ℱk\mathop{\downarrow}\mathcal{F}for the finite set{k↓F∣F∈ℱ}\{k\mathop{\downarrow}F\mid F\in\mathcal{F}\}of finite covers ofkk.

The following computation is straightforward.

## Lemma 3.5.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice, and letℱ\mathcal{F}be a finite set of finite covers ofl∈Ll\in L. Then for eachh∈Lh\in Lwe haveh​(l∣ℱ)=⋁k∈h↓l(k∣k↓ℱ)∈ℤ​Lh(l\mid\mathcal{F})=\bigvee_{k\in h\mathop{\downarrow}l}(k\mid k\mathop{\downarrow}\mathcal{F})\in\mathbb{Z}L.

Let us come back to the motivating case whereG≅S⋉XG\cong S\ltimes XandU:L→𝒪c​(X)U\colon L\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)is anSS-equivariant generating representation of a locally finite weak semilatticeLLonXX. IdentifyingCc​(L^,ℤ)C_{c}(\widehat{L},\mathbb{Z})withℤ​L\mathbb{Z}L, our description of the kernelIIof the surjectionCc​(L^,ℤ)↠Cc​(X,ℤ)C_{c}(\widehat{L},\mathbb{Z})\twoheadrightarrow C_{c}(X,\mathbb{Z})using (3.1) is now equivalent to the statement thatIIis generated by the relative complements(l∣F)(l\mid F)of certain finite covers inLL. Consider, for eachl∈Ll\in L,ℛ​(l)={F⊆L|F​is a finite cover of​l,(l∣F)∈I}.\mathscr{R}(l)=\left\{F\subseteq L\;|\;F\text{ is a finite cover of }l,\,(l\mid F)\in I\right\}.

- (M)

is an element ofℛ(s.l)\mathscr{R}(s.l). ThisSS-invariance holds becauseIIisSS-invariant. Let us now turn this into an abstract definition.

## Definition 3.6.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice. Asystem of finite covers inLLis a tupleℛ=(ℛ​(l))l∈L\mathscr{R}=(\mathscr{R}(l))_{l\in L}

where eachℛ​(l)\mathscr{R}(l)is a set of finite covers ofl∈Ll\in L, such that
- •

for eachl∈Ll\in L, the trivial cover{l}\{l\}is an element ofℛ​(l)\mathscr{R}(l), and
- •

ℛ\mathscr{R}satisfies condition (M): if for alll∈Ll\in L,F∈ℛ​(l)F\in\mathscr{R}(l)andk∈Lk\in Lwithk≤lk\leq l, we have thatk↓F∈ℛ​(k)k\mathop{\downarrow}F\in\mathscr{R}(k).

Given an actionS↷LS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Lof an inverse semigroupSS, we say thatℛ\mathscr{R}isSS-invariant if for alll∈Ll\in L,s∈Ss\in Swithl∈dom⁡(s)l\in\operatorname{dom}(s), and for allF∈ℛ​(l)F\in\mathscr{R}(l), we have thats.F:-{s.f|f∈F}s.F\coloneq\left\{s.f\;|\;f\in F\right\}lies inℛ(s.l)\mathscr{R}(s.l).

The setℛ​(l)\mathscr{R}(l)of all finite coversFFofllfor which(l∣F)∈I(l\mid F)\in I, i.e.l=⋃f∈Ffl=\bigcup_{f\in F}finXX, may be impractically large. In concrete applications there is often a natural system of finite covers which is large enough to generateIIbut small enough to be manageable.

## 3.3.A recursive construction

Now let us present a systematic way of constructing independent resolutions. SupposeL0L_{0}is a locally finite weak semilattice and thatℛ0\mathscr{R}_{0}is a system of finite covers ofL0L_{0}.

## Definition 3.10.

The locally finite weak semilattice of relative complementsL1=LL0,ℛ0L_{1}=L_{L_{0},\mathscr{R}_{0}}associated to(L0,ℛ0)(L_{0},\mathscr{R}_{0})is the following set of idempotents inℤ​L0\mathbb{Z}L_{0}:L1:-{(l∣ℱ)|l∈L0,∅≠ℱ⊆ℛ0​(l)​finite}∪{0}.L_{1}\coloneq\left\{(l\mid\mathcal{F})\;|\;l\in L_{0},\,\emptyset\neq\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathscr{R}_{0}(l)\text{ finite}\right\}\cup\left\{0\right\}.

Given(l∣ℱ)∈L1(l\mid\mathcal{F})\in L_{1}andF′∈ℛ0​(l)F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{R}_{0}(l), setR​(l​∣ℱ∣​F′)\displaystyle R(l\mid\mathcal{F}\mid F^{\prime}):-{(l∣ℱ∪{F′})}∪{(f∣f↓ℱ)|f∈F′},\displaystyle\coloneq\{(l\mid\mathcal{F}\cup\left\{F^{\prime}\right\})\}\cup\left\{(f\mid f\mathop{\downarrow}\mathcal{F})\;|\;f\in F^{\prime}\right\},ℛ1​(l∣ℱ)\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{1}(l\mid\mathcal{F}):-ℛL0,ℛ0​(l∣ℱ):-{R​(l​∣ℱ∣​F′)|F′∈ℛ0​(l)}.\displaystyle\coloneq\mathscr{R}_{L_{0},\mathscr{R}_{0}}(l\mid\mathcal{F})\coloneq\left\{R(l\mid\mathcal{F}\mid F^{\prime})\;|\;F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{R}_{0}(l)\right\}.

## Proposition 3.11.

L1L_{1}is again a locally finite weak semilattice with the order coming from the idempotents inℤ​L0\mathbb{Z}L_{0}, with the↓\mathop{\downarrow}operation given for(k∣ℰ),(l∣ℱ)∈L1(k\mid\mathcal{E}),(l\mid\mathcal{F})\in L_{1}by(k∣ℰ)↓(l∣ℱ)={(f∣f↓(ℰ∪ℱ))|f∈k↓l}.(k\mid\mathcal{E})\mathop{\downarrow}(l\mid\mathcal{F})=\left\{(f\mid f\mathop{\downarrow}(\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}))\;|\;f\in k\mathop{\downarrow}l\right\}.

For each(l∣ℱ)∈L1(l\mid\mathcal{F})\in L_{1}andF′∈ℛ0​(l)F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{R}_{0}(l)the setR​(l​∣ℱ∣​F′)R(l\mid\mathcal{F}\mid F^{\prime})is a finite cover of(l∣ℱ)(l\mid\mathcal{F})whose join inℤ​L0\mathbb{Z}L_{0}is(l∣ℱ∪{F′})∨⋁f∈F′(f∣f↓ℱ)=(l∣ℱ).(l\mid\mathcal{F}\cup\{F^{\prime}\})\vee\bigvee_{f\in F^{\prime}}(f\mid f\mathop{\downarrow}\mathcal{F})=(l\mid\mathcal{F}).

Henceℛ1\mathscr{R}_{1}is a system of finite covers inL1L_{1}.

## Proof.

## 4.Discretisation

Now we come to the crucial idea of discretisation. LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice equipped with an action of an inverse semigroupSS. WriteL×:-L∖{0}L^{\times}\coloneq L\setminus\left\{0\right\}, and consider the restrictionS↷L×S\mathbin{\curvearrowright}L^{\times}of our action toL×L^{\times}. ViewingL×L^{\times}as a discrete topological space, we viewS↷L×S\mathbin{\curvearrowright}L^{\times}as an action ofSSby partial homeomorphisms onL×L^{\times}and form the partial transformation groupoidS⋉L×S\ltimes L^{\times}.

The idea of discretisation is to find a suitable weak equivalence betweenS⋉L×S\ltimes L^{\times}andS⋉L^S\ltimes\widehat{L}.

Note that we always have an embeddingL×↪L^L^{\times}\hookrightarrow\widehat{L}sendingk∈L×k\in L^{\times}to the characterχk\chi_{k}given byχk​(l)=1\chi_{k}(l)=1if and only ifk≤lk\leq l. Hence we obtain an induced embeddingS⋉L×↪S⋉L^S\ltimes L^{\times}\hookrightarrow S\ltimes\widehat{L}. But this is not the desired weak equivalence. Instead, we construct a proper étale correspondenceΩ:S⋉L×→S⋉L^\Omega\colon S\ltimes L^{\times}\to S\ltimes\widehat{L}.

To define étale correspondences, we need the notion of an action of an étale groupoidGGon a topological spaceXX. Such an action consists of ananchor mapτ:X→G0\tau\colon X\to G^{0}assumed continuous, and anaction map(g,x)↦g.x:G×G0X={(g,x)∈G×X∣s(g)=τ(x)}→X(g,x)\mapsto g.x\colon G\times_{G^{0}}X=\{(g,x)\in G\times X\mid s(g)=\tau(x)\}\to Xsatisfyingτ(g.x)=r(g)\tau(g.x)=r(g),τ​(x).x=x\tau(x).x=xand(gh).x=g.(h.x)(gh).x=g.(h.x)whenever the expressions make sense. The action is said to befreeifg.x=xg.x=ximpliesg∈G0g\in G^{0},properifG×G0X→X×XG\times_{G^{0}}X\to X\times X,(g,x)↦(g.x,x)(g,x)\mapsto(g.x,x)is proper, andétaleif the anchor map is a local homeomorphism.
The action groupoidG⋉XG\ltimes Xis an étale groupoid with arrow spaceG×G0XG\times_{G^{0}}Xand unit spaceXXwith multiplication given by(g,h.x)(h,x)=(gh,x)(g,h.x)(h,x)=(gh,x).

## Definition 4.1(Étale correspondence, see[1]).

LetGGandHHbe étale groupoids. AGG-HHbispaceis a topological spaceΩ\Omegaequipped with a left action ofGGand a right action ofHHthat commute; that is, the left anchorρ:Ω→G0\rho\colon\Omega\to G^{0}isHH-invariant, the right anchorσ:Ω→H0\sigma\colon\Omega\to H^{0}isGG-invariant andg.(ω.h)=(g.ω).hg.(\omega.h)=(g.\omega).hfor compatibleg∈Gg\in G,ω∈Ω\omega\in\Omegaandh∈Hh\in H. Anétale correspondenceΩ:G→H\Omega\colon G\to His aGG-HHbispaceΩ\Omegasuch that the right actionΩ↶H\Omega\mathbin{\curvearrowleft}His free, proper and étale.
The correspondenceΩ\Omegaisproperif the mapΩ/H→G0\Omega/H\to G^{0}induced by the left anchor is proper. IfG=G0G=G^{0}andH=H0H=H^{0}are locally compact Hausdorff spaces, the data of an étale correspondence reduces to a locally compact Hausdorff spaceZZequipped with a continuous mapρ:Z→X\rho\colon Z\to Xand a local homeomorphismσ:Z→Y\sigma\colon Z\to Y; we refer toZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Yas atopological correspondence. Note thatZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Yis proper if and only ifρ:Z→X\rho\colon Z\to Xis proper.

Let us introduce the following notation.

## Definition 4.2.

LetGGbe an étale groupoid, letX1X_{1}andX2X_{2}be right and leftGG-spaces respectively with anchorsσ:X1→G0\sigma\colon X_{1}\to G^{0}andρ:X2→G0\rho\colon X_{2}\to G^{0}and letK⊆GK\subseteq Gbe an open subgroupoid. Thefibre productofX1X_{1}andX2X_{2}overKKX1×KX2:-{(x1,x2)∈X1×X2∣σ​(x1)=ρ​(x2)∈K0}/KX_{1}\times_{K}X_{2}\coloneq\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in X_{1}\times X_{2}\mid\sigma(x_{1})=\rho(x_{2})\in K^{0}\}/K

is the quotient defined by(x1.g,x2)∼(x1,g.x2)(x_{1}.g,x_{2})\sim(x_{1},g.x_{2})forx1∈X1x_{1}\in X_{1},x2∈X2x_{2}\in X_{2}andg∈Kg\in Kwithσ​(x1)=r​(g)\sigma(x_{1})=r(g)andρ​(x2)=s​(g)\rho(x_{2})=s(g). We equipX1×KX2X_{1}\times_{K}X_{2}with the quotient topology and write[x1,x2][x_{1},x_{2}]for the class of a pair(x1,x2)(x_{1},x_{2}). IfK1,K2⊆GK_{1},K_{2}\subseteq Gare open subgroupoids with actionsX1↶K1↷X2↶K2↷X3X_{1}\mathbin{\curvearrowleft}K_{1}\mathbin{\curvearrowright}X_{2}\mathbin{\curvearrowleft}K_{2}\mathbin{\curvearrowright}X_{3}such that the left and right actions onX2X_{2}commute, we identify(X1×K1X2)×K2X3(X_{1}\times_{K_{1}}X_{2})\times_{K_{2}}X_{3}withX1×K1(X2×K2X3)X_{1}\times_{K_{1}}(X_{2}\times_{K_{2}}X_{3})via[[x1,x2],x3]↦[x1,[x2,x3]][[x_{1},x_{2}],x_{3}]\mapsto[x_{1},[x_{2},x_{3}]]and write[x1,x2,x3]∈X1×K1X2×K2X3[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]\in X_{1}\times_{K_{1}}X_{2}\times_{K_{2}}X_{3}.

## Remark 4.3.

In the above,X1X_{1}andX2X_{2}may be viewed as partialKK-spaces in that the anchor maps toK0K^{0}are only defined on some open subset.

The compositionΛ∘Ω:G→K\Lambda\circ\Omega\colon G\to Kof étale correspondencesΩ:G→H\Omega\colon G\to HandΛ:H→K\Lambda\colon H\to Kis given by theGG-HHbispaceΩ×HΛ\Omega\times_{H}\Lambda. Étale correspondences between étale groupoids form a(2,1)(2,1)-category, with isomorphisms of bispaces as22-morphisms. In this paper we shall only apply properties of the ordinary category whose morphisms are isomorphism classes of étale correspondences, thus we often work with étale correspondences (sometimes implicitly) only up to isomorphism.

## Definition 4.4.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice. The (topological)discretisation correspondenceΩ0:L×→L^\Omega^{0}\colon L^{\times}\to\widehat{L}is the topological correspondenceΩ0=∐l∈L×L^​(l)={(l,χ)∈L××L^|χ​(l)=1},\Omega^{0}=\coprod_{l\in L^{\times}}\widehat{L}(l)=\left\{(l,\chi)\in L^{\times}\times\widehat{L}\;|\;\chi(l)=1\right\},

with left anchor mapρ​(l,χ)=l\rho(l,\chi)=land right anchor mapσ​(l,χ)=χ\sigma(l,\chi)=\chi. The discretisation correspondence is proper becauseL^​(l)\widehat{L}(l)is compact for eachl∈L×l\in L^{\times}.

WhenLLis equipped with an action of an inverse semigroupSS, the discretisation correspondenceΩ0\Omega^{0}also inherits an action ofSSwithdomΩ0⁡(s)=∐l∈domL×⁡(s)L​(l)\operatorname{dom}_{\Omega^{0}}(s)=\coprod_{l\in\operatorname{dom}_{L^{\times}}(s)}L(l)for eachs∈Ss\in Sands.(l,χ)=(s.l,s.χ)s.(l,\chi)=(s.l,s.\chi). The discretisation correspondenceΩ0:L×→L^\Omega^{0}\colon L^{\times}\to\widehat{L}becomes anSS-equivariant topological correspondence in the following sense.

## Definition 4.5.

LetSSbe an inverse semigroup. AnSS-equivariant topological correspondenceis a topological correspondenceZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Yequipped with actions ofSSonXX,YYandZZ, such that the anchor mapsρ:Z→X\rho\colon Z\to Xandσ:Z→Y\sigma\colon Z\to YareSS-equivariant withρ−1​(domX⁡(s))=domZ⁡(s)\rho^{-1}(\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s))=\operatorname{dom}_{Z}(s)for eachs∈Ss\in S.

The composition of twoSS-equivariant topological correspondences is again anSS-equivariant topological correspondence.
The condition thatρ−1​(domX⁡(s))=domZ⁡(s)\rho^{-1}(\operatorname{dom}_{X}(s))=\operatorname{dom}_{Z}(s)for eachs∈Ss\in Sensures well-definition of the naïve action ofS⋉XS\ltimes XonZZ.

## Lemma 4.6.

LetSSbe an inverse semigroup and letZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Ybe anSS-equivariant topological correspondence. ThenS⋉XS\ltimes Xacts onZZwith anchorρ:Z→X\rho\colon Z\to Xvia[s,x].z=s.z[s,x].z=s.zso that(S⋉X)⋉Z=S⋉Z(S\ltimes X)\ltimes Z=S\ltimes Z.

This means thatS⋉X↷S⋉ZS\ltimes X\mathbin{\curvearrowright}S\ltimes Zgiven by[s,x].[t,z]=[s​t,z][s,x].[t,z]=[st,z]defines an action correspondenceS⋉X→S⋉ZS\ltimes X\to S\ltimes Z. Composing this with the étale correspondence associated to the étale homomorphism[s,z]↦[s,σ​(z)]:S⋉Z→S⋉Y[s,z]\mapsto[s,\sigma(z)]\colon S\ltimes Z\to S\ltimes Y, we obtain the following étale correspondence.

## Definition 4.7.

LetSSbe an inverse semigroup and letZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Ybe anSS-equivariant topological correspondence. Theassociated étale correspondenceZ​(S):S⋉X→S⋉YZ(S)\colon S\ltimes X\to S\ltimes Yhas underlying spaceZ​(S)=Z×Y(S⋉Y)Z(S)=Z\times_{Y}(S\ltimes Y), with left action given by[s,x].(z,[t,y])=(s.z,[st,y])[s,x].(z,[t,y])=(s.z,[st,y])and right action by(z,[s,y]).[t,y′]=(z,[s​t,y′])(z,[s,y]).[t,y^{\prime}]=(z,[st,y^{\prime}]). Forg=[s,x]g=[s,x]andz∈Zz\in Zwithρ​(z)=x\rho(z)=x, we writeg|z=[s,σ​(z)]g|_{z}=[s,\sigma(z)], which is the unique element ofS⋉YS\ltimes Ysatisfyingg.(z,σ(z))=(g.z,g|z)=(g.z,σ(z)).g|z∈Z(S)g.(z,\sigma(z))=(g.z,g|_{z})=(g.z,\sigma(z)).g|_{z}\in Z(S).

Note thatZ​(S):S⋉X→S⋉YZ(S)\colon S\ltimes X\to S\ltimes Yis proper if and only ifZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Yis proper. The construction of the étale correspondence associated to anSS-equivariant topological correspondence is functorial with respect to composition of correspondences.

## Definition 4.8.

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice equipped with the action of an inverse semigroupSS. Thediscretisation correspondenceΩ:S⋉L×→S⋉L^\Omega\colon S\ltimes L^{\times}\to S\ltimes\widehat{L}is the étale correspondenceΩ=Ω0​(S)\Omega=\Omega^{0}(S)associated to theSS-equivariant topological discretisation correspondenceΩ0:L×→L^\Omega^{0}\colon L^{\times}\to\widehat{L}. As a space, we haveΩ=∐l∈L×{[s,χ]∈S⋉L^|χ∈domL^⁡(s),s.χ∈L^​(l)}.\Omega=\coprod_{l\in L^{\times}}\left\{[s,\chi]\in S\ltimes\widehat{L}\;|\;\chi\in\operatorname{dom}_{\widehat{L}}(s),\,s.\chi\in\widehat{L}(l)\right\}.

It is proper becauseΩ0\Omega^{0}is proper.

## Remark 4.9.

We haveΩ≅Ω0×L^(S⋉L^)\Omega\cong\Omega^{0}\times_{\widehat{L}}(S\ltimes\widehat{L}). However,Ω\Omegacannot be described as(S⋉L×)×L×Ω0(S\ltimes L^{\times})\times_{L^{\times}}\Omega^{0}. The reason is that for[l,s,χ]∈Ω[l,s,\chi]\in\Omega,llmight not lie inranL×⁡(s)\operatorname{ran}_{L^{\times}}(s). For the same reason, the canonical actionS↷Ω0S\mathbin{\curvearrowright}\Omega^{0}does not describeΩ\OmegaasS⋉Ω0S\ltimes\Omega^{0}.

## 4.1.Discretisation in groupoid homology

LetLLbe a locally finite weak semilattice equipped with an action of an inverse semigroupSS. As explained in[49, Corollary 3.6],Ω\Omegainduces the mapH∗⁡(Ω):H∗⁡(S⋉L×)→H∗⁡(S⋉L^)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(\Omega)\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes L^{\times})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\widehat{L})in groupoid homology. The argument for the following result is identical to that in[49, Example 3.10]. We reproduce it here for convenience.

## Theorem 4.10.

For every inverse semigroup actionS↷LS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Lon a locally finite weak semilatticeLL, the discretisation correspondenceΩ\Omegafrom Definition4.8induces an isomorphism in groupoid homologyH∗⁡(Ω):H∗⁡(S⋉L×)→∼H∗⁡(S⋉L^).\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(\Omega)\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes L^{\times})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\widehat{L}).

## Proof.

The étale correspondenceΩ:S⋉L×→S⋉L^\Omega\colon S\ltimes L^{\times}\to S\ltimes\widehat{L}decomposes into the correspondences associated to the actionS⋉L×↷Ω0S\ltimes L^{\times}\mathbin{\curvearrowright}\Omega^{0}and the étale homomorphismS⋉Ω0→S⋉L^S\ltimes\Omega^{0}\to S\ltimes\widehat{L}. SetG=S⋉L×G=S\ltimes L^{\times}andH=S⋉L^H=S\ltimes\widehat{L}andK=S⋉Ω0K=S\ltimes\Omega^{0}. Following[49, Examples 3.8 and 3.9], the induced mapH∗⁡(Ω)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(\Omega)is induced by a chain mapΦ:ℤ​[G∙]→ℤ​[H∙]\Phi\colon\mathbb{Z}[G^{\bullet}]\to\mathbb{Z}[H^{\bullet}]of Matui’s chain complexes. The chain map is induced atn≥0n\geq 0(contravariantly) by the mapKn→Gn,[sn−1,…,s0,l,χ]↦[sn−1,…,s0,l]K^{n}\to G^{n},[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},l,\chi]\mapsto[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},l]and (covariantly) by the local homeomorphismKn→Hn,[sn−1,…,s0,l,χ]↦[sn−1,…,s0,χ]K^{n}\to H^{n},[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},l,\chi]\mapsto[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},\chi]. This boils down to the mapΦn:ℤ​[Gn]\displaystyle\Phi_{n}\colon\mathbb{Z}[G^{n}]→ℤ​[Hn]\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}[H^{n}]δx\displaystyle\delta_{x}↦1Vx,\displaystyle\mapsto 1_{V_{x}},

where for eachx=[sn−1,…,s0,l]∈Gnx=[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},l]\in G^{n}, the compact open setVx⊆HnV_{x}\subseteq H^{n}is given byVx={[sn−1,…,s0,χ]∣χ∈L^​(l)}.V_{x}=\{[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},\chi]\mid\chi\in\widehat{L}(l)\}.

We will show thatΦn\Phi_{n}is in fact an isomorphism for eachn≥0n\geq 0.

Forx,y∈Gnx,y\in G^{n}we setx≤yx\leq yifVx⊆VyV_{x}\subseteq V_{y}, this happens exactly ifx=[sn−1,…,s0,k]x=[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},k]andy=[sn−1,…,s0,l]y=[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},l]withk≤lk\leq l. It follows that{Vx∣x≤y}\{V_{x}\mid x\leq y\}is a generating independent concrete locally finite weak semilattice inVyV_{y}. By Lemma2.16, the restrictionℤ​[{x∣x≤y}]→ℤ​[Vy]\mathbb{Z}[\{x\mid x\leq y\}]\to\mathbb{Z}[V_{y}]ofΦn\Phi_{n}is an isomorphism. Surjectivity ofΦn\Phi_{n}follows because{Vy∣y∈Gn}\{V_{y}\mid y\in G^{n}\}coversHnH^{n}and hence{ℤ​[Vy]∣y∈Gn}\{\mathbb{Z}[V_{y}]\mid y\in G^{n}\}spansℤ​[Hn]\mathbb{Z}[H^{n}].

For injectivity ofΦn\Phi_{n}, letF⊆GnF\subseteq G^{n}be finite and let(ax)x∈F(a_{x})_{x\in F}be integers with∑x∈Fax​1Vx=0\sum_{x\in F}a_{x}1_{V_{x}}=0. Pick a maximal elementy=[sn−1,…,s0,l]∈Fy=[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},l]\in F. Then0=∑x∈Fax​1Vx0=\sum_{x\in F}a_{x}1_{V_{x}}evaluated at the element[sn−1,…,s0,χl]∈Hn[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},\chi_{l}]\in H^{n}recoversaya_{y}because[sn−1,…,s0,χl]∈Vx[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},\chi_{l}]\in V_{x}if and only ify≤xy\leq x.
