SinceS⋉L×S\ltimes L^{\times}is a discrete groupoid, it is Morita equivalent to a disjoint union of isotropy groups. Therefore, we arrive at the following consequence.

## Corollary 4.11.

For every inverse semigroup actionS↷LS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Lon a locally finite weak semilatticeLL, we have the following isomorphism in groupoid homology:⨁[l]∈S\L×H∗⁡((S⋉L×)ll)≅H∗⁡(S⋉L^).\bigoplus_{[l]\in S\backslash L^{\times}}\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}((S\ltimes L^{\times})_{l}^{l})\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\widehat{L}).

HereS\L×S\backslash L^{\times}is the set of orbits for the actionS↷L×S\mathbin{\curvearrowright}L^{\times}, and(S⋉L×)ll(S\ltimes L^{\times})_{l}^{l}denotes the isotropy at the unitl∈L×=(S⋉L×)0l\in L^{\times}=(S\ltimes L^{\times})^{0}.

## 4.2.Discretisation in K-theory

The discretisation correspondenceΩ:S⋉L×→S⋉L^\Omega\colon S\ltimes L^{\times}\to S\ltimes\widehat{L}induces a properC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondenceC∗⁡(Ω):C∗⁡(S⋉L×)→C∗⁡(S⋉L^)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\Omega)\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(S\ltimes L^{\times})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(S\ltimes\widehat{L}). In fact, becauseΩ\Omegais built from theSS-equivariant topological correspondenceΩ0:L×→L^\Omega^{0}\colon L^{\times}\to\widehat{L}, this descends to a properC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondenceCr∗⁡(Ω):Cr∗⁡(S⋉L×)→Cr∗⁡(S⋉L^)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(\Omega)\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes L^{\times})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes\widehat{L})at the reduced level (see PropositionA.1).
Our goal is to prove that this induces an isomorphism in K-theoryK∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(S⋉L×))→∼K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(S⋉L^))\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes L^{\times}))\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes\widehat{L})).

## Theorem 4.12.

LetLLbe a countable locally finite weak semilattice equipped with the action of a countable inverse semigroupSSand suppose thatS⋉L^S\ltimes\widehat{L}is Hausdorff. Then the discretisation correspondenceΩ\Omegainduces an isomorphismK∗top​(Ω):K∗top​(S⋉L×)→∼K∗top​(S⋉L^)\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(\Omega)\colon\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(S\ltimes L^{\times})\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(S\ltimes\widehat{L})

of the left-hand side of the Baum–Connes conjecture for the groupoidsS⋉L×S\ltimes L^{\times}andS⋉L^S\ltimes\widehat{L}. IfS⋉L^S\ltimes\widehat{L}andS⋉L×S\ltimes L^{\times}satisfy the Baum–Connes conjecture, thenCr∗⁡(Ω):Cr∗⁡(S⋉L×)→Cr∗⁡(S⋉L^)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(\Omega)\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes L^{\times})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes\widehat{L})induces an isomorphismK∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(Ω)):K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(S⋉L×))→∼K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(S⋉L^)).\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(\Omega))\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes L^{\times}))\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes\widehat{L})).

The following is the analogue of Corollary4.11(and the argument is the same).

## Corollary 4.13.

LetLLandSSbe as in Theorem4.12. Under the same hypotheses as in Theorem4.12, we have the following isomorphism in K-theory:⨁[l]∈S\L×K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡((S⋉L×)ll))≅K∗⁡(S⋉L^).\bigoplus_{[l]\in S\backslash L^{\times}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}((S\ltimes L^{\times})_{l}^{l}))\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(S\ltimes\widehat{L}).

HereS\L×S\backslash L^{\times}is the set of orbits for the actionS↷L×S\mathbin{\curvearrowright}L^{\times}, and(S⋉L×)ll(S\ltimes L^{\times})_{l}^{l}denotes the isotropy at the unitl∈L×=(S⋉L×)0l\in L^{\times}=(S\ltimes L^{\times})^{0}.

## Remark 4.14.

S⋉L×S\ltimes L^{\times}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture if and only if, for every unitl∈L×=(S⋉L×)0l\in L^{\times}=(S\ltimes L^{\times})^{0}, the isotropy group(S⋉L×)ll(S\ltimes L^{\times})_{l}^{l}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture. The reason is thatS⋉L×S\ltimes L^{\times}is Morita equivalent to the groupoid∐[l]∈S\L×(S⋉L×)ll\coprod_{[l]\in S\backslash L^{\times}}(S\ltimes L^{\times})_{l}^{l}, and satisfying the Baum–Connes conjecture is preserved under Morita equivalence. The latter fact is well known, but we could not find an explicit reference. It follows for example from the functoriality results in[50]. It was stated before[50, Corollary 7.4]that because each isotropy group inS⋉E×S\ltimes E^{\times}embeds in an isotropy group ofS⋉E^S\ltimes\widehat{E}, the former groupoid inherits satisfaction of the Baum–Connes conjecture from the latter. The second-named author would like to take the opportunity to correct this mistake;[50, Corollary 7.4 and Example 7.6]should include the extra hypothesis that the relevant subgroups satisfy the Baum–Connes conjecture.

The proof of Theorem4.12is long and technical, so let us first summarise the main ideas. Section5is dedicated to the proof.
Satisfaction of the Baum–Connes conjecture reduces the problem from operator K-theory to topological K-theory. The main tool we will apply is[50, Corollary 7.1], which gives a sufficient condition for the map (see[50, Example 5.11])K∗top​(Ω):K∗top​(S⋉L×)→K∗top​(S⋉L^)\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(\Omega)\colon\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(S\ltimes L^{\times})\to\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(S\ltimes\widehat{L})

induced byΩ\Omegato be an isomorphism. SettingX=L×X=L^{\times},Y=L^Y=\widehat{L},G=S⋉X=S⋉L×G=S\ltimes X=S\ltimes L^{\times}andH=S⋉Y=S⋉L^H=S\ltimes Y=S\ltimes\widehat{L}, this condition asks that for eachn≥0n\geq 0, an induced map of derived functors(4.1)𝕃n​(IndΩ,αΩ,fΩ):𝕃nℱG​K∗⁡(G⋉C0​(X))→𝕃nℱH​K∗⁡(H⋉C0​(Y))\mathbb{L}_{n}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\Omega},\alpha_{\Omega},f_{\Omega})\colon\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\mathcal{F}_{G}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(G\ltimes C_{0}(X))\to\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\mathcal{F}_{H}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(H\ltimes C_{0}(Y))

with respect to certain familiesℱG\mathcal{F}_{G}andℱH\mathcal{F}_{H}of proper groupoids is an isomorphism.

The derived functors and the functoriality appearing in (4.1) require a considerable amount of KK-theoretic machinery from[50](and references therein[7,48,45,46,44,57]) to formulate, which we cover in Section5.1. Morally speaking, the group𝕃nℱG​K∗⁡(G⋉C0​(X))\mathbb{L}_{n}^{\mathcal{F}_{G}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(G\ltimes C_{0}(X))is thennth homology group ofGGfor some non-standard homology theory of groupoids. Indeed, for Hausdorff ample groupoidsGGwith torsion-free isotropy, this will coincide with ordinary groupoid homologyHn⁡(G)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{n}(G)for∗=0*=0and vanish for∗=1*=1(this is the case already covered in[50]). For now, it suffices to say that𝕃n​(IndΩ,αΩ,fΩ)\mathbb{L}_{n}(\operatorname{Ind}_{\Omega},\alpha_{\Omega},f_{\Omega})is the map induced in homology by a particular chain map(4.2)Φ:K∗⁡(G⋉P∙)→K∗⁡(H⋉Q∙)\Phi\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(G\ltimes P_{\bullet})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(H\ltimes Q_{\bullet})

involving certain chain complexesP∙P_{\bullet}andQ∙Q_{\bullet}ofGG-C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras andHH-C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras.

In Section5.2, we provide convenient groupoid models for theC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebrasG⋉PnG\ltimes P_{n}andH⋉QnH\ltimes Q_{n}appearing in (4.2), and provide concrete étale groupoid correspondences which induce the chain mapΦ\Phi.

In Section5.3, we show that these étale groupoid correspondences modellingΦ\Phican themselves be expressed as discretisation correspondences where the acting inverse semigroup is finite. In this situation, we can prove an isomorphism in K-theory in an elementary fashion, completing the proof of Theorem4.12.

## 5.Proof of K-theoretic discretisation

## 5.1.KK-theoretic preliminaries

Here we provide a summary of the construction from[50]of the derived functors appearing in (4.1). The construction takes place within the Meyer–Nest framework of homological algebra in triangulated categories[45,46,44], utilising the triangulated structure on the Kasparov categoryKKG\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{G}of a second countable Hausdorff étale groupoidGG[7, Section 1.1]. The derived functors are the left derived functors of the K-theory functorK∗(G⋉−):KKG→Ab\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(G\ltimes-)\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{G}\to\operatorname{Ab}with respect to a countable familyℱ\mathcal{F}of proper open subgroupoids ofGGsatisfying condition (P)[50, §3]; for everyx∈G0x\in G^{0}and every finite subgroupΓ≤Gxx\Gamma\leq G^{x}_{x}of the isotropy groupGxxG^{x}_{x}, there is a memberK∈ℱK\in\mathcal{F}withΓ⊆K\Gamma\subseteq K. Before spelling out exactly what being a derived functor with respect toℱ\mathcal{F}means, let us describe a canonical choice of the familyℱ\mathcal{F}from[50, Example 3.6]for the transformation groupoidG=S⋉XG=S\ltimes Xof the action of an inverse semigroupSSon a locally compact Hausdorff spaceXX.
For eache∈E×={e∈S∣e2=e,e≠0}e\in E^{\times}=\{e\in S\mid e^{2}=e,e\neq 0\}and finite subgroupF⊆Se:-{s∈S|s​e​s−1=e}F\subseteq S_{e}\coloneq\left\{s\in S\;|\;ses^{-1}=e\right\}, setG​(F)\displaystyle G(F):-{[s,l]∈G|s∈F,l∈domX⁡(e)}.\displaystyle\coloneq\left\{[s,l]\in G\;|\;s\in F,\,l\in\operatorname{dom}_{X}(e)\right\}.

SettingF↓={s​d|s∈F,d∈E}={s​d|s∈F,d∈E,d≤e}F_{\mathop{\downarrow}}=\left\{sd\;|\;s\in F,\,d\in E\right\}=\left\{sd\;|\;s\in F,\,d\in E,\,d\leq e\right\}, which is the inverse semigroup generated byFFand{d∈E∣d≤e}\{d\in E\mid d\leq e\}, the actionS↷XS\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Xrestricts to an actionF↓↷domX⁡(e)F_{\mathop{\downarrow}}\mathbin{\curvearrowright}\operatorname{dom}_{X}(e), and we have a canonical isomorphismG​(F)≅F↓⋉domX⁡(e)G(F)\cong F_{\mathop{\downarrow}}\ltimes\operatorname{dom}_{X}(e). Note thatG​(F)G(F)is a compact open subgroupoid ofGG. ## Proposition 5.1.

LetSSbe a countable inverse semigroup and letZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Ybe a properSS-equivariant topological correspondence withXX,YYandZZsecond countable andG=S⋉XG=S\ltimes XandH=S⋉YH=S\ltimes YHausdorff.

Supposef~:P∙→IndZ​(S)⁡Q∙\tilde{f}\colon P_{\bullet}\to\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(S)}Q_{\bullet}is a chain map inKKG\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{G}which sits overfZ​(S):C0​(X)→IndZ​(S)⁡C0​(Y)f_{Z(S)}\colon C_{0}(X)\to\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(S)}C_{0}(Y)such that the compositionΦ:K∗⁡(G⋉P∙)→K∗⁡(G⋉f~)K∗⁡(G⋉IndZ​(S)⁡Q∙)→αZ​(S)​(Q∙)K∗⁡(H⋉Q∙)\Phi\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(G\ltimes P_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(G\ltimes\tilde{f})}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(G\ltimes\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(S)}Q_{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\alpha_{Z(S)}(Q_{\bullet})}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(H\ltimes Q_{\bullet})

is an isomorphism of chain complexes. ThenK∗top​(Z​(S)):K∗top​(G)→K∗top​(H)\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}({Z(S)})\colon\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(G)\to\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(H)is an isomorphism.

## 5.2.Concrete groupoid models for maps

Let us identify a concrete groupoid model for the chain map (5.5).

We first consider the chain mapP∙→IndZ​(S)⁡Q∙P_{\bullet}\to\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(S)}Q_{\bullet}, whose components are elements inKKG\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{G}. We will model these elements using proper topological correspondences that are equivariant with respect toGG.

## Definition 5.2.

LetGGbe an étale groupoid. AGG-equivariant topological correspondenceis a topological correspondenceZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Yequipped with actions ofGGonXX,YYandZZsuch that the anchor maps areGG-equivariant. The transformation groupoidG⋉ZG\ltimes Zinherits the structure of an étale correspondenceG⋉Z:G⋉X→G⋉YG\ltimes Z\colon G\ltimes X\to G\ltimes Ywith anchorsρ∘r\rho\circ randσ∘s\sigma\circ sand actions given by(g1,x).(g2,z)=(g1​g2,z)(g_{1},x).(g_{2},z)=(g_{1}g_{2},z)and(g1,z).(g2,y)=(g1g2,g2−1.z)(g_{1},z).(g_{2},y)=(g_{1}g_{2},g_{2}^{-1}.z).

We describe the construction of aGG-equivariantC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondence from aGG-equivariant topological correspondence in AppendixA, where we moreover considerGG-equivariant étale correspondences and establish compatibility with crossed products and composition. In particular, properGG-equivariant topological correspondencesZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Yfunctorially induce morphismsC0​(X)→C0​(Y)C_{0}(X)\to C_{0}(Y)inKKG\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{G}by PropositionA.5.

## Lemma 5.5.

LetG=S⋉WG=S\ltimes Wbe an étale groupoid arising from the action of an inverse semigroupSSon a locally compact Hausdorff spaceWW. Then anyGG-equivariant topological correspondenceZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Yis anSS-equivariant topological correspondence, with the actions ofSSonXX,YYandZZinduced by the actions ofGG. Moreover, the induced étale correspondenceZ​(S):S⋉X→S⋉YZ(S)\colon S\ltimes X\to S\ltimes Yis isomorphic toG⋉Z:G⋉X→G⋉YG\ltimes Z\colon G\ltimes X\to G\ltimes Y.

with anchor mapsρ(n)\rho^{(n)}andσ(n)\sigma^{(n)}as given in (5.2):

is isomorphic to the étale correspondence induced by theSS-equivariant topological correspondence (5.13)Z​(Fn,…,F0):X​(Fn,…,F0)→Y​(Fn,…,F0).Z(F_{n},\dotsc,F_{0})\colon X(F_{n},\dotsc,F_{0})\to Y(F_{n},\dotsc,F_{0}).

Let us summarize our findings.

described in (5.16) induces aK∗\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}-isomorphism. ThenK∗top​(Z​(S)):K∗top​(G)→K∗top​(H)\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(Z(S))\colon\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(G)\to\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(H)is an isomorphism.

By PropositionA.7,G⋉f~:G⋉Pn→G⋉IndZ​(S)⁡QnG\ltimes\tilde{f}\colon G\ltimes P_{n}\to G\ltimes\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(S)}Q_{n}is modelled at the groupoid level by the étale correspondencesG⋉Z​(Fn,…,F0):G⋉X​(Fn,…,F0)→G⋉(Z×YY​(Fn,…,F0))G\ltimes Z(F_{n},\dots,F_{0})\colon G\ltimes X(F_{n},\dots,F_{0})\to G\ltimes(Z\times_{Y}Y(F_{n},\dots,F_{0})). Moreover, the induction correspondenceZ​(S)⋉Qn:G⋉IndZ​(S)⁡Qn→H⋉Qn{Z(S)}\ltimes Q_{n}\colon G\ltimes\operatorname{Ind}_{Z(S)}Q_{n}\to H\ltimes Q_{n}is modelled by theSS-equivariant local homeomorphism[z,y]↦y:Z×YY​(Fn,…,F0)→Y​(Fn,…,F0)[z,y]\mapsto y\colon Z\times_{Y}Y(F_{n},\dotsc,F_{0})\to Y(F_{n},\dotsc,F_{0})described in (5.4). By Lemma5.6the compositionG⋉Pn→H⋉QnG\ltimes P_{n}\to H\ltimes Q_{n}is modelled by theSS-equivariant topological correspondenceZ​(Fn,…,F0):X​(Fn,…,F0)→Y​(Fn,…,F0)Z(F_{n},\dotsc,F_{0})\colon X(F_{n},\dotsc,F_{0})\to Y(F_{n},\dotsc,F_{0})described in (5.13). Finally, by Lemma5.7the resulting map in K-theory (5.5)K∗⁡(G⋉Pn)→K∗⁡(H⋉Qn)\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(G\ltimes P_{n})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(H\ltimes Q_{n})is isomorphic to the map in K-theory induced by theFn,↓F_{n,\mathop{\downarrow}}-equivariant topological correspondenceZn:Xn→YnZ_{n}\colon X_{n}\to Y_{n}described in (5.16). ## 5.3.Isomorphism at the level of ABC spectral sequences

Let us for the rest of this subsection fix a countable locally finite weak semilatticeLLwith an action of a countable inverse semigroupSSwith idempotent semilatticeEEsuch thatS⋉L^S\ltimes\widehat{L}is Hausdorff. LetG=S⋉L×G=S\ltimes L^{\times}andH=S⋉L^H=S\ltimes\widehat{L}withX=L×X=L^{\times}andY=L^Y=\widehat{L}, and letΩ=Ω0​(S):G→H\Omega=\Omega^{0}(S)\colon G\to Hbe the discretisation correspondence. Our goal now is to prove the following.

## Theorem 5.9.

The proper étale correspondenceZn​(Fn,↓):Fn,↓⋉Xn→Fn,↓⋉YnZ_{n}(F_{n,\mathop{\downarrow}})\colon F_{n,\mathop{\downarrow}}\ltimes X_{n}\to F_{n,\mathop{\downarrow}}\ltimes Y_{n}induces aK∗\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}-isomorphism.

For the proof, let us first analyse the space𝒴n:-HHn0×Hn−10…×H10H×H00H0.\mathscr{Y}_{n}\coloneq{}_{H_{n}^{0}}H\times_{H_{n-1}^{0}}\dotso\times_{H_{1}^{0}}H\times_{H_{0}^{0}}H^{0}.

Considerℒn:-{(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k))⊆𝒴n|sm∈em+1​S​em​for0≤m≤n−1,k∈domL⁡(sn−1​⋯​s0)},\mathscr{L}_{n}\coloneq\left\{(s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k))\subseteq\mathscr{Y}_{n}\;|\;s_{m}\in e_{m+1}Se_{m}\text{ for $0\leq m\leq n-1$ },k\in\operatorname{dom}_{L}(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{0})\right\},

where(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k)):-{[sn−1,…,s0,χ]∈𝒴n|χ∈L^​(k)}.(s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k))\coloneq\left\{[s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\chi]\in\mathscr{Y}_{n}\;|\;\chi\in\widehat{L}(k)\right\}.

We note that by construction each member(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k))∈ℒn(s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k))\in\mathscr{L}_{n}is compact and open, and homeomorphic to its projectionL^​(k)\widehat{L}(k)ontoH0H^{0}.

## Lemma 5.10.

The familyℒn\mathscr{L}_{n}is a generating independent locally finite weak semilattice in𝒴n\mathscr{Y}_{n}in the sense of Definition2.10.

In particular, we have an isomorphismC∗⁡(ℒn)≅C0​(𝒴n),e↦1e\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\mathscr{L}_{n})\cong C_{0}(\mathscr{Y}_{n}),\,e\mapsto 1_{e}, which induces the identification𝒴n≅ℒn^\mathscr{Y}_{n}\cong\widehat{\mathscr{L}_{n}}.

## Proof.

To see thatℒn\mathscr{L}_{n}is a weak semilattice, takee1,e2∈ℒne_{1},e_{2}\in\mathscr{L}_{n}. Thene1∩e2e_{1}\cap e_{2}is a compact subset of𝒴n\mathscr{Y}_{n}(this uses Hausdorffness). Moreover, it is easy to see that every point of𝒴n\mathscr{Y}_{n}has a neighbourhood given by an element ofℒn\mathscr{L}_{n}which is completely contained ine1∩e2e_{1}\cap e_{2}. It follows thate1∩e2e_{1}\cap e_{2}can be written as a finite unione1∩e2=⋃ifie_{1}\cap e_{2}=\bigcup_{i}f_{i}for somefi∈ℒnf_{i}\in\mathscr{L}_{n}. Now taked∈ℒnd\in\mathscr{L}_{n}withd=(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k))d=(s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k))such thatd⊆e1∩e2=⋃ifid\subseteq e_{1}\cap e_{2}=\bigcup_{i}f_{i}. As[sn−1,…,s0,χk][s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\chi_{k}]lies indd, this element has to lie in somefif_{i}. Suppose thatfi=(tn−1,…,t0,L^​(l))f_{i}=(t_{n-1},\dotsc,t_{0},\widehat{L}(l)). Thenχk​(l)=1\chi_{k}(l)=1, so thatk≤lk\leq landL^​(k)⊆L^​(l)\widehat{L}(k)\subseteq\widehat{L}(l). Moreover,[sn−1,…,s0,χk]=[tn−1,…,t0,χk][s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\chi_{k}]=[t_{n-1},\dotsc,t_{0},\chi_{k}]implies that there are idempotentsem∈E×e_{m}\in E^{\times}withsm​em=tm​ems_{m}e_{m}=t_{m}e_{m}andsm−1​⋯​s0.k=tm−1​⋯​t0.k∈domL×⁡(em)s_{m-1}\dotsm s_{0}.k=t_{m-1}\dotsm t_{0}.k\in\operatorname{dom}_{L^{\times}}(e_{m})for all0≤m≤n−10\leq m\leq n-1. It follows that[sn−1,…,s0,χ]=[tn−1,…,t0,χ][s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\chi]=[t_{n-1},\dotsc,t_{0},\chi]for allχ∈L^​(k)\chi\in\widehat{L}(k), i.e.,d⊆fid\subseteq f_{i}.

To see thatℒn\mathscr{L}_{n}is locally finite, take a finite subsetF⊆ℒnF\subseteq\mathscr{L}_{n}. Proceeding inductively on#​F\#F, we want to show that there exists a↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed finite subset\widebar​F\widebar{F}ofℒn\mathscr{L}_{n}containingFF. So suppose we have found such a\widebar​F\widebar{F}forFF. Now take an additional elementf∈ℒnf\in\mathscr{L}_{n}, sayf=(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k))f=(s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k)). The down-set{g∈ℒn∣g≤f}\{g\in\mathscr{L}_{n}\mid g\leq f\}carries the weak semilattice structure of{l∈L∣l≤k}\{l\in L\mid l\leq k\}. In particular, sinceLLis locally finite, we may a finite subsetA⊆{g∈ℒn∣g≤f}A\subseteq\{g\in\mathscr{L}_{n}\mid g\leq f\}which is↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed and contains the finite set{f}∪(\widebar​F↓f)\{f\}\cup(\widebar{F}\mathop{\downarrow}f). Now we claim that\widebar​F∪A\widebar{F}\cup Ais↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed. Indeed, givene∈\widebar​Fe\in\widebar{F}andg∈Ag\in A, we havee↓g=e↓(f↓g)=(e↓f)↓ge\mathop{\downarrow}g=e\mathop{\downarrow}(f\mathop{\downarrow}g)=(e\mathop{\downarrow}f)\mathop{\downarrow}g, which lies inAAbecausee↓f⊆Ae\mathop{\downarrow}f\subseteq AandAAis↓\mathop{\downarrow}-closed.

The familyℒn\mathscr{L}_{n}satisfies (GEN) because for each fixed(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k))(s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k)), the family{(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(l))|l≤k}\left\{(s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},\widehat{L}(l))\;|\;l\leq k\right\}

satisfies (GEN) for(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k))(s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k)). Moreover,ℒn\mathscr{L}_{n}satisfies (IND). Indeed, given elementse,e1,…,em∈ℒne,e_{1},\dots,e_{m}\in\mathscr{L}_{n}withe=⋃i=1meie=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}e_{i}wheree=(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k))e=(s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k)), then there is some1≤i≤m1\leq i\leq mwith[sn−1,…,s0,χk]∈ei⊆e[s_{n-1},\dots,s_{0},\chi_{k}]\in e_{i}\subseteq e, from which it follows thatei=ee_{i}=e.

Given(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k))∈ℒn(s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k))\in\mathscr{L}_{n}, we let[sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k)][s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k)]denote its image inYnY_{n}with respect to the canonical projection𝒴n↠Yn\mathscr{Y}_{n}\twoheadrightarrow Y_{n}. We setLn:-{[sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k)]⊆Yn|(sn−1,…,s0,L^​(k))∈ℒn}.L_{n}\coloneq\left\{[s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k)]\subseteq Y_{n}\;|\;(s_{n-1},\dotsc,s_{0},\widehat{L}(k))\in\mathscr{L}_{n}\right\}.

The following is a consequence of Lemma5.10.

## Lemma 5.11.

Therefore, if[d]⊆[l1]∩[l2][d]\subseteq[l_{1}]\cap[l_{2}], then there aref1,f2∈Ff_{1},f_{2}\in Fwithd⊆l1f1∩l2f2d\subseteq l_{1}^{f_{1}}\cap l_{2}^{f_{2}}, and hencedf2−1⊆l1f1​f2−1∩l2d^{f_{2}^{-1}}\subseteq l_{1}^{f_{1}f_{2}^{-1}}\cap l_{2}. It follows thatLnL_{n}taken with the inclusion order is a weak semilattice. It is moreover locally finite, because ifA⊆LnA\subseteq L_{n}is a finite subset, the preimageB={b∈ℒn∣[b]∈A}B=\{b\in\mathscr{L}_{n}\mid[b]\in A\}is finite, and its closure\widebar​B\widebar Bunder↓\mathop{\downarrow}remains finite. To see thatLnL_{n}satisfies (IND), supposed,d1,…,dm∈ℒnd,d_{1},\dots,d_{m}\in\mathscr{L}_{n}with[d]=⋃i=1m[di][d]=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}[d_{i}]. The compact open subsets ofYnY_{n}correspond directly toFF-invariant compact open subsets of𝒴n\mathscr{Y}_{n}. k\in\operatorname{dom}_{L^{\times}}(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{0}),\,\chi\in\widehat{L}(k)\end{array}\right\},

## Remark 5.14.

LetLLbe a countable locally finite weak semilattice equipped with the action of a countable inverse semigroupSSand suppose thatS⋉L^S\ltimes\widehat{L}is Hausdorff. Assume that, for every unitl∈L×=(S⋉L×)(0)l\in L^{\times}=(S\ltimes L^{\times})^{(0)}, the isotropy group(S⋉L×)ll(S\ltimes L^{\times})_{l}^{l}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture. ThenS⋉L^S\ltimes\widehat{L}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture if and only if the discretisation correspondenceΩ\Omegainduces an isomorphismK∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(Ω)):K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(S⋉L×))→∼K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(S⋉L^)).\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(\Omega))\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes L^{\times}))\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S\ltimes\widehat{L})).

Indeed, our assumptions imply thatS⋉L×S\ltimes L^{\times}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture by Remark4.14. Hence the implication “⇒\Rightarrow” follows from Theorem4.12. For “⇐\Leftarrow”, note that, in the diagram (5.21), the mapμS⋉L×\mu_{S\ltimes L^{\times}}is an isomorphism by our assumptions, and thatK∗top​(Ω)\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(\Omega)is an isomorphism by Theorem4.12. Hence, as (5.21) commutes,μS⋉L^\mu_{S\ltimes\widehat{L}}is an isomorphism if and only ifK∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(Ω))\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(\Omega))is an isomorphism, as claimed.

## 6.Applications and examples

Let us now address the question how independent resolutions help to compute or understand groupoid homology and K-theory for reduced groupoidC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras. We then apply our results to concrete examples.

Assume now that we are given an independent resolutionGi=Si⋉Li^G_{i}=S_{i}\ltimes\widehat{L_{i}}of an ample groupoidGG. If our groupoidsGiG_{i}are exact, then the independent resolution yields a long exact sequence of reduced groupoidC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras, and the above discussion applies toAi=Cr∗⁡(Gi)A_{i}=\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(G_{i}). If furthermore allSiS_{i}andLiL_{i}are countable, allGiG_{i}are Hausdorff, andGiG_{i}andSi⋉Li×S_{i}\ltimes L^{\times}_{i}satisfy the Baum–Connes conjecture, then Corollary4.13allows to compute K-theory ofCr∗⁡(Gi)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(G_{i})in terms of K-groups of isotropy groups, which are typically easier to handle. Here is a first direct consequence.

## Corollary 6.2.

Assume that we are given a finite length independent resolutionGi=Si⋉Li^G_{i}=S_{i}\ltimes\widehat{L_{i}}of an ample groupoidGG, and suppose that for everyii, the groupoidsGiG_{i}are Hausdorff and exact, andSiS_{i}as well asLiL_{i}are countable. Further assume that for everyiiand every unitl∈Li×=(Si⋉Li×)(0)l\in L_{i}^{\times}=(S_{i}\ltimes L_{i}^{\times})^{(0)}, the isotropy group(Si⋉Li×)ll(S_{i}\ltimes L_{i}^{\times})_{l}^{l}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture, and that for everyii, the discretisation correspondenceΩi:Si⋉Li×→Si⋉Li^\Omega_{i}\colon S_{i}\ltimes L_{i}^{\times}\to S_{i}\ltimes\widehat{L_{i}}from §4induces an isomorphismK∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(Ωi)):K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(Si⋉Li×))→∼K∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(Si⋉L^i))\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(\Omega_{i}))\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S_{i}\ltimes L_{i}^{\times}))\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(S_{i}\ltimes\widehat{L}_{i})). ThenGGsatisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture.

## Proof.

Since our groupoidsGiG_{i}are exact, our finite length independent resolution yields a long exact sequence of reduced groupoidC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras, which splits into finitely many short exact sequences, which in turn induce finitely many long exact sequences in K-theory. We obtain analogous long exact sequences for the left-hand side of the Baum–Connes conjecture, i.e., inK∗top\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{top}}_{*}(see[70, § 7.1]and[71, § 5]). By naturality of these long exact sequences, induction on the length of our independent resolution shows that ifGiG_{i}satisfy the Baum–Connes conjecture for allii, then so doesGG. By Remark5.14, our assumptions imply thatGiG_{i}satisfy the Baum–Connes conjecture for allii, which completes the proof.
∎

In the following, let us develop a concrete application. We need the following terminology: Given a countable discrete groupΓ\Gamma, we say that the discretisation formula with arbitrary coefficients is valid forΓ\Gammaif for every countable semilatticeEE, everyΓ\Gamma-actionΓ↷E\Gamma\mathbin{\curvearrowright}Eby order automorphisms and every separableΓ\Gamma-C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebraAA, we have an isomorphism(6.5)K∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(E×)))≅K∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(E^))).\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(E^{\times})))\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(\widehat{E}))).

HereΓ\Gammaacts via diagonal actions onA⊗C0​(E×)A\otimes C_{0}(E^{\times})andA⊗C0​(E^)A\otimes C_{0}(\widehat{E}).

## Corollary 6.3.

LetΓ\Gammabe a countable discrete group. Assume thatΓ\Gammais exact. Suppose that there exists a finite length independent resolution forG−1=ΓG_{-1}=\Gammaconsisting of groupoidsGiG_{i}of the formGi=Γ⋉E^iG_{i}=\Gamma\ltimes\widehat{E}_{i}for countable semilatticesEiE_{i}with the property that, for alle∈Ei×e\in E^{\times}_{i}, the stabilizer groupΓe\Gamma_{e}is finite. ThenΓ\Gammasatisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficients if and only if the discretisation formula with arbitrary coefficients is valid forΓ\Gamma.

## Proof.

The implication “⇒\Rightarrow” follows from[15, Theorem 3.12]. For the converse, we use the following equivalent formulation of the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficients. As pointed out in[17, § 5.3], a countable discrete groupΓ\Gammasatisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficients if and only if the following statement is true: For every separableΓ\Gamma-C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebraAA, ifK∗⁡(F⋉rA)=0\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(F\ltimes_{r}A)=0for every finite subgroupF⊆ΓF\subseteq\Gamma(here theFF-action onAAis given by restricting theΓ\Gamma-action onAA), thenK∗⁡(Γ⋉rA)=0\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}A)=0. Now suppose thatAAis a separableGG-C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebra such thatK∗⁡(F⋉rA)=0\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(F\ltimes_{r}A)=0for every finite subgroupF⊆ΓF\subseteq\Gamma. SinceΓ\Gammais exact, our finite length independent resolution forΓ\Gammaproduces an exact sequence(6.6)0→Ak→…→A2→A1→A0→Γ⋉rA→0,0\to A_{k}\to\dotso\to A_{2}\to A_{1}\to A_{0}\to\Gamma\ltimes_{r}A\to 0,

where eachAiA_{i}is of the formAi=Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(Ei^))A_{i}=\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(\widehat{E_{i}})). Using (6.5) and the analogue of Corollary4.13, we deduce thatK∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(Ei^)))≅⨁[e]∈Γ\Ei×K∗⁡(Γe⋉rA).\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(\widehat{E_{i}})))\cong\bigoplus_{[e]\in\Gamma\backslash E^{\times}_{i}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma_{e}\ltimes_{r}A).

SinceΓe\Gamma_{e}is a finite subgroup ofΓ\Gammaby assumption, it follows thatK∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(Ei^)))=0\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(\widehat{E_{i}})))=0for all0≤i≤k0\leq i\leq k. Exactness of (6.6) then implies thatK∗⁡(Γ⋉rA)=0\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}A)=0, as desired.
∎

## Remark 6.4.

LetΓ\Gammabe a countable discrete group. Suppose that there exists a finite length independent resolution forG−1=ΓG_{-1}=\Gammaconsisting of groupoidsGi=Γ⋉E^iG_{i}=\Gamma\ltimes\widehat{E}_{i}as in Corollary6.3with the property that, for alle∈Ei×e\in E^{\times}_{i}, the stabilizer groupΓe\Gamma_{e}is finite. Then the same is true for any subgroupΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}ofΓ\Gamma. Indeed, we just have to restrict theΓ\Gamma-action onEiE_{i}toΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}and observe thatGi′:-Γ′⋉E^iG^{\prime}_{i}\coloneq\Gamma^{\prime}\ltimes\widehat{E}_{i}is an independent resolution forG−1′=Γ′G^{\prime}_{-1}=\Gamma^{\prime}(Γe′\Gamma^{\prime}_{e}is finite becauseΓe′⊆Γe\Gamma^{\prime}_{e}\subseteq\Gamma_{e}).

Here is an alternative approach to Corollary6.3. We first need the following.

## Remark 6.5.

Suppose thatΓ\Gammais a countable discrete group acting on a countable semilatticeEE. In this situation, an element𝒙∈KKΓ⁡(C0​(E×),C0​(E^))\bm{x}\in\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{\Gamma}(C_{0}(E^{\times}),C_{0}(\widehat{E}))was constructed in[15, § 3]such that the induced mapK∗⁡(Γ⋉r𝒙):K∗⁡(Γ⋉rC0​(E×))→K∗⁡(Γ⋉rC0​(E^))\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}\bm{x})\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}C_{0}(E^{\times}))\to\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}C_{0}(\widehat{E}))in K-theory can be identified with the mapK∗⁡(Cr∗⁡(Ω))\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}_{r}}(\Omega))induced by the discretisation correspondenceΩ:Γ⋉E×→Γ⋉E^\Omega\colon\Gamma\ltimes E^{\times}\to\Gamma\ltimes\widehat{E}from §4.

With this preparation, we can now state the following applications.

## Corollary 6.6.

LetΓ\Gammabe a countable discrete group. Assume thatΓ\Gammais exact. Suppose that there exists a finite length independent resolution forG−1=ΓG_{-1}=\Gammaconsisting of groupoidsGiG_{i}of the formGi=Γ⋉E^iG_{i}=\Gamma\ltimes\widehat{E}_{i}for countable semilatticesEiE_{i}.
- (i)

Suppose that, for everyiiand everye∈Ei×e\in E^{\times}_{i}, the stabilizer groupΓe\Gamma_{e}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture, and that for everyii, the element𝒙i∈KKΓ⁡(C0​(Ei×),C0​(Ei^))\bm{x}_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{\Gamma}(C_{0}(E_{i}^{\times}),C_{0}(\widehat{E_{i}}))as in Remark6.5induces an isomorphismK∗⁡(Γ⋉r𝒙i):K∗⁡(Γ⋉rC0​(Ei×))→∼K∗⁡(Γ⋉rC0​(Ei^))\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}\bm{x}_{i})\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}C_{0}(E_{i}^{\times}))\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}C_{0}(\widehat{E_{i}})). ThenΓ\Gammasatisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture.
- (ii)

LetAAbe a separableΓ\Gamma-C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebra. Suppose that, for everyiiand everye∈Ei×e\in E^{\times}_{i}, the stabilizer groupΓe\Gamma_{e}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficientAA, and that for everyii, the element𝒙i∈KKΓ⁡(C0​(Ei×),C0​(Ei^))\bm{x}_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{\Gamma}(C_{0}(E_{i}^{\times}),C_{0}(\widehat{E_{i}}))as in Remark6.5induces an isomorphismK∗⁡(Γ⋉r([idA]⊗ℂ𝒙i)):K∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(Ei×)))→∼K∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(Ei^)))\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}([{\rm id}_{A}]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\bm{x}_{i}))\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(E_{i}^{\times})))\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(\widehat{E_{i}}))). ThenΓ\Gammasatisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficientAA.
- (iii)

The following are equivalent:
- (a)

For everyiiand everye∈Ei×e\in E^{\times}_{i}, the stabilizer groupΓe\Gamma_{e}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture with arbitrary coefficients, and for everyiiand every separableΓ\Gamma-C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebraAA, the element𝒙i∈KKΓ⁡(C0​(Ei×),C0​(Ei^))\bm{x}_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{\Gamma}(C_{0}(E_{i}^{\times}),C_{0}(\widehat{E_{i}}))as in Remark6.5induces an isomorphismK∗⁡(Γ⋉r([idA]⊗ℂ𝒙i)):K∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(Ei×)))→∼K∗⁡(Γ⋉r(A⊗C0​(Ei^)))\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}([{\rm id}_{A}]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\bm{x}_{i}))\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(E_{i}^{\times})))\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-3.01389pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\sim$}}\operatorname{\mathrm{K}}_{*}(\Gamma\ltimes_{r}(A\otimes C_{0}(\widehat{E_{i}}))).
- (b)

Γ\Gammasatisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture with arbitrary coefficients.

## Proof.

The proof of (i) is analogous to the one of Corollary6.2. The proof of (ii) is also similar, adding the coefficient algebraAA. For (iii), (a)⇒\Rightarrow(b) follows from (ii), and (b)⇒\Rightarrow(a) follows from[15, Theorem 3.12]because the Baum–Connes conjecture with arbitrary coefficients passes to subgroups by[10, Theorem 2.5].
∎

## 6.3.Examples

Garside categories provide a source for examples of groupoids which admit finite length independent resolutions. Let us first give a brief introduction to Garside categories, which in this article will mean small categories with Garside families with particular properties as specified below.444The notion of a Garside category is in general somewhat flexible; the emphasis is on studying Garside families with various desirable properties, but different situations merit different requirements on the Garside family.We will follow the exposition in[36]and refer the reader to[18,35,36]and the references therein for more details.

Letℭ\mathfrak{C}be a small category. The set of objectsℭ0\mathfrak{C}^{0}inℭ\mathfrak{C}will be viewed as a subset of the set of morphisms by identifying an object with the corresponding identity morphism. Moreover, we will identify the category with its set of morphisms, again denoted byℭ\mathfrak{C}. Let𝔡:ℭ→ℭ0\mathfrak{d}:\>\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}^{0}and𝔱:ℭ→ℭ0\mathfrak{t}:\>\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}^{0}be the domain and target maps.ℭ∗\mathfrak{C}^{*}denotes the set of invertible elements ofℭ\mathfrak{C}. We need to introduce some terminology.

## Definition 6.7.


- •

ℭ\mathfrak{C}is called left cancellative, if, for allc,x,y∈ℭc,x,y\in\mathfrak{C}with𝔡​(c)=𝔱​(x)=𝔱​(y)\mathfrak{d}(c)=\mathfrak{t}(x)=\mathfrak{t}(y),c​x=c​ycx=cyimpliesx=yx=y.
- •

ℭ\mathfrak{C}is called finitely aligned if for alla,b∈ℭa,b\in\mathfrak{C}, there exists a finite subset{ci}⊆ℭ\left\{c_{i}\right\}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}such thata​ℭ∩b​ℭ=⋃ici​ℭa\mathfrak{C}\cap b\mathfrak{C}=\bigcup_{i}c_{i}\mathfrak{C}.
- •

Givena,b∈ℭa,b\in\mathfrak{C}, we writea⪯ba\preceq bifaais a left divisor ofbb, i.e.,b∈a​ℭb\in a\mathfrak{C}. We writea≺ba\prec bifb​ℭ⊊a​ℭb\mathfrak{C}\subsetneq a\mathfrak{C}. We writea​⪯∼ba\mathop{\preceq}\limits^{\vbox to-5.00305pt{\kern-4.2194pt\hbox{$\sim$}\vss}}bifaais a right divisor ofbb, i.e.,b∈ℭ​ab\in\mathfrak{C}a. We writea​≺∼ba\mathop{\prec}\limits^{\vbox to-5.00305pt{\kern-4.2194pt\hbox{$\sim$}\vss}}bifℭ​b⊊ℭ​a\mathfrak{C}b\subsetneq\mathfrak{C}a. We writea=∗ba=^{*}bifa∈b​ℭ∗a\in b\mathfrak{C}^{*}(which is equivalent toa​ℭ=b​ℭa\mathfrak{C}=b\mathfrak{C}).
- •

A subset𝔖⊆𝔖\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}is closed under right comultiples if for allr,s∈𝔖r,s\in\mathfrak{S}anda∈ℭa\in\mathfrak{C}withr⪯ar\preceq a,s⪯as\preceq a, there existst∈𝔖t\in\mathfrak{S}withr⪯tr\preceq t,s⪯ts\preceq tandt⪯at\preceq a.

Now we can introduce the concept of a Garside family.

## Definition 6.8.

Suppose𝔖⊆ℭ\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}is closed under right comultiples such that𝔖∪ℭ∗\mathfrak{S}\cup\mathfrak{C}^{*}generatesℭ\mathfrak{C}and𝔖♯:-𝔖​ℭ∗∪ℭ∗\mathfrak{S}^{\sharp}\coloneq\mathfrak{S}\mathfrak{C}^{*}\cup\mathfrak{C}^{*}is closed under right divisors.
- •

A finite sequences1,s2,…s_{1},s_{2},\dotscinℭ\mathfrak{C}is called apathif𝔡​(sk)=𝔱​(sk+1)\mathfrak{d}(s_{k})=\mathfrak{t}(s_{k+1})for allkk. Such a path will be denoted bys1​s2​⋯s_{1}s_{2}\dotsm. A paths1,…,sl∈𝔖♯s_{1},\dotsc,s_{l}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\sharp}is called normal if for all1≤k≤l−11\leq k\leq l-1andr∈𝔖r\in\mathfrak{S}, ifr⪯sk​sk+1r\preceq s_{k}s_{k+1}thenr⪯skr\preceq s_{k}.
- •

Fora∈ℭa\in\mathfrak{C}, a normal decomposition or normal form ofaais given by a normal paths1​…​sls_{1}\dotsc s_{l}in𝔖♯\mathfrak{S}^{\sharp}witha=s1​⋯​sla=s_{1}\dotsm s_{l}.
- •

𝔖\mathfrak{S}is called aGarside familyif every element inℭ\mathfrak{C}admits a normal decomposition.

In the following, assume thatℭ\mathfrak{C}is a finitely aligned left cancellative small category, and that𝔖⊆ℭ\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}is a Garside family. Further suppose that the following hold:
- •

𝔖\mathfrak{S}is=∗=^{*}-transverse, i.e., for alls1,s2∈𝔖s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathfrak{S},s1=∗s2s_{1}=^{*}s_{2}impliess1=s2s_{1}=s_{2}.
- •

𝔖∩ℭ∗=∅\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathfrak{C}^{*}=\emptyset.
- •

𝔖\mathfrak{S}is locally finite, i.e.,#​𝔳​𝔖<∞\#\mathfrak{v}\mathfrak{S}<\inftyfor all𝔳∈ℭ0\mathfrak{v}\in\mathfrak{C}^{0}.
- •

For allℓ≥1\ell\geq 1,(𝔖≤ℓ)♯(\mathfrak{S}^{\leq\ell})^{\sharp}is closed under left divisors. Here𝔖≤ℓ:-𝔖∪𝔖2∪…∪𝔖ℓ\mathfrak{S}^{\leq\ell}\coloneq\mathfrak{S}\cup\mathfrak{S}^{2}\cup\dotso\cup\mathfrak{S}^{\ell}.

Note thatL:-{a​ℭ∣a∈ℭ}∪{0}L\coloneq\{a\mathfrak{C}\mid a\in\mathfrak{C}\}\cup\left\{0\right\}is a weak semilattice (with respect to inclusion as partial order) becauseℭ\mathfrak{C}is finitely aligned. Moreover,LLis locally finite because our assumptions imply (see the discussion in[36, § 3]) that, for allℓ≥1\ell\geq 1,𝔖≤ℓ\mathfrak{S}^{\leq\ell}is↓\downarrow-closed and locally finite (i.e.#​𝔳​𝔖≤ℓ<∞\#\mathfrak{v}\mathfrak{S}^{\leq\ell}<\inftyfor all𝔳∈ℭ0\mathfrak{v}\in\mathfrak{C}^{0}). LetSSbe the left inverse hull ofℭ\mathfrak{C}as in[35,36], which acts as an inverse semigroup onLLin the natural way.
LetXXbe a locally compact Hausdorff totally disconnected space with an action ofSSand anSS-equivariant generating representationU:L→𝒪c​(X)U\colon L\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(X)ofLL. Note that via Lemma2.11, the data of(X,U)(X,U)corresponds to a closed invariant subspace ofL^\widehat{L}. Fora∈ℭa\in\mathfrak{C}let(6.7)ℛ​(a​ℭ):={F⊆a​𝔖|⋃b∈FU​(b​ℭ)=U​(a​ℭ)}∪{{a​ℭ}}.\mathscr{R}(a\mathfrak{C}):=\left\{F\subseteq a\mathfrak{S}\;|\;\bigcup_{b\in F}U(b\mathfrak{C})=U(a\mathfrak{C})\right\}\cup\{\{a\mathfrak{C}\}\}.

It follows from[36, Lemma 3.8]that the systemℛ\mathscr{R}of concrete finite covers inLLis thorough. It isSS-invariant by construction.

Uniform finiteness ofℛ​(a​ℭ)\mathscr{R}(a\mathfrak{C})is ensured ifsupv∈ℭ0#​v​𝔖<∞\sup_{v\in\mathfrak{C}^{0}}\#\,v\mathfrak{S}<\infty.
Hence the construction in §3.3produces a finite length independent resolution ofS⋉XS\ltimes X.

Examples6.9,6.10as well as the examples discussed in6.3.1below are special cases.

## 6.3.1.Garside monoids and their enveloping groups

Let us recall the definition of Garside monoids from[18, Chapter I, § 2.1].666We note that this particular notion of a Garside monoid is more specific than that of a monoid viewed as a small category equipped with a Garside family with the finiteness assumptions we take above. For example, monoids associated to self-similar group actions have non-trivial invertible elements, which are disallowed here by the length functionλ\lambda.A Garside monoid consists of a monoidMMwhich is cancellative, admits a functionλ:M→ℕ\lambda\colon M\to\mathbb{N}such thatλ​(x​y)≥λ​(x)+λ​(y)\lambda(xy)\geq\lambda(x)+\lambda(y)andλ​(x)>0\lambda(x)>0for allx∈M∖{1}x\in M\setminus\{1\}, such that any two elements ofMMhave a left- and right-lcm and a left- and right-gcd, together with an elementΔ∈M\Delta\in Mwhose left- and right-divisors coincide and generateMM, such that the setD={d∈M∣Δ∈d​M}D=\{d\in M\mid\Delta\in dM\}of divisors ofΔ\DeltainMMis finite. LetΓ\Gammabe the enveloping group ofMM. Note thatΓ\Gammais given by the group of (left- or right-) fractions forMM, i.e.,Γ=M−1​M=M​M−1\Gamma=M^{-1}M=MM^{-1}.

LetE0E_{0}be the semilatticeΓ∪{0}\Gamma\cup\left\{0\right\}, where we identify an elementγ∈Γ\gamma\in\Gammawith the subsetγ​M\gamma MofΓ\Gammaand the partial order is given by inclusion. To see thatE0E_{0}is indeed a semilattice, takeγ,ζ∈Γ\gamma,\zeta\in\Gamma, writeγ−1​ζ=x−1​y\gamma^{-1}\zeta=x^{-1}yfor somex,y∈Mx,y\in Mand letzzbe the right-lcm ofxxandyy. Now we compute(γ​M)∩(ζ​M)=γ​(M∩γ−1​ζ​M)=γ​(M∩x−1​y​M)=γ​x−1​((x​M)∩(y​M))=γ​x−1​z​M∈E0.(\gamma M)\cap(\zeta M)=\gamma(M\cap\gamma^{-1}\zeta M)=\gamma(M\cap x^{-1}yM)=\gamma x^{-1}((xM)\cap(yM))=\gamma x^{-1}zM\in E_{0}.

Γ\Gammaacts on the semilatticeE0E_{0}by order automorphisms via left multiplication. Also consider the singleton space{∗}\{*\}with trivialΓ\Gamma-action. The trivial representationU:E0→𝒪c​({∗})U\colon E_{0}\to\mathcal{O}_{c}(\{*\})withU​(γ)={∗}U(\gamma)=\{*\}for eachγ∈Γ\gamma\in\Gammais (trivially)Γ\Gamma-equivariant and generating, but far from independent. However,E0E_{0}admits the following thoroughΓ\Gamma-invariant systemℛ\mathscr{R}of concrete finite covers with respect toUU. The semilatticesEjE_{j}look as follows:E0\displaystyle E_{0}=Γ∪{0},\displaystyle=\Gamma\cup\left\{0\right\},E1\displaystyle E_{1}={(γ∣D1)|γ∈Γ;∅≠D1⊆D}∪{0},\displaystyle=\left\{(\gamma\mid D_{1})\;|\;\gamma\in\Gamma;\,\emptyset\neq D_{1}\subseteq D\right\}\cup\left\{0\right\},E2\displaystyle E_{2}={(γ​∣D1∣​D2)|γ∈Γ;∅≠D1,D2⊆D​disjoint}∪{0},\displaystyle=\left\{(\gamma\mid D_{1}\mid D_{2})\;|\;\gamma\in\Gamma;\,\emptyset\neq D_{1},D_{2}\subseteq D\text{ disjoint}\right\}\cup\left\{0\right\},…,\displaystyle\dotso,Ej\displaystyle E_{j}={(γ​∣D1∣​⋯∣Dj)|γ∈Γ;∅≠D1,…,Dj⊆D​disjoint}∪{0},\displaystyle=\left\{(\gamma\mid D_{1}\mid\cdots\mid D_{j})\;|\;\gamma\in\Gamma;\,\emptyset\neq D_{1},\dotsc,D_{j}\subseteq D\text{ disjoint}\right\}\cup\left\{0\right\},…\displaystyle\dotso

with a similar recursive definition as in Example3.16. Note that this is a finite length independent resolution, because the length is at most#​D−1\#\,D-1. The following is now a consequence of Corollary6.3and Remark6.4.

## Corollary 6.11.

LetΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}be a subgroup of the enveloping group of a Garside monoid. Assume thatΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}is exact. ThenΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}satisfies the Baum–Connes conjecture with arbitrary coefficients if and only if the discretisation formula with arbitrary coefficients is valid forΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}.

Let us now present concrete examples. First, recall that we introduced Artin–Tits groups in §1.

## Example 6.12.

An Artin–Tits monoid is a Garside monoid in the sense of[18, Chapter I, § 2.1]if and only it is spherical. The corresponding enveloping groups, spherical Artin–Tits groups, are special classes of Artin–Tits groups. Concrete examples are given by Braid monoids and Braid groups. Spherical Artin–Tits groups are linear by[12,19]and thus exact by[28]. Hence Corollary6.11implies that for a subgroupΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}of a spherical Artin–Tits group, the Baum–Connes conjecture with coefficients is true forΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}if and only if the discretisation formula with coefficients is valid forΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}.

## 6.3.2.Groupoids corresponding to Stein’s groups

This class of examples appears also in, for instance,[33](see also[68],[69]and[37, § 2.2.7]). LetΠ\Pibe a multiplicative subgroup ofℝ>0\mathbb{R}_{>0}andA+A_{+}be the monoidA∩[0,∞)A\cap[0,\infty)for some subgroupA⊆(ℝ,+)A\subseteq(\mathbb{R},+). Assume thatΠ\Piacts onA+A_{+}by multiplication. LetSSbe the left inverse hull of thea​x+bax+b-monoidA+⋊ΠA_{+}\rtimes\PiandEEits semilattice of idempotents. We have a decompositionE^=Ω∐{∞}\widehat{E}=\Omega\amalg\left\{\infty\right\}, whereΩ\Omegais anSS-invariant open, non-compact subset such thatS⋉ΩS\ltimes\Omegais minimal. We are interested in a restriction of the groupoidS⋉ΩS\ltimes\Omegato a non-empty compact open subset ofΩ\Omega; let us call itGG. Its topological full group has been studied in[65]and can be viewed as generalizations of Thompson’s groupVV, where we vary the conditions on slopes and breakpoints as well as the length of the underlying interval (choosing different lengths corresponds to restrictingS⋉ΩS\ltimes\Omegato different compact open subsets). This description of Stein’s groups as topological full groups has been worked out in[68], based on ideas in[33], and the reader may consult[68, § 7.2]for details. The groupoidGGwe are interested in is Morita equivalent toS⋉ΩS\ltimes\OmegabecauseS⋉ΩS\ltimes\Omegais minimal. For this reason, if we are interested in groupoid homology ofGG, it suffices to study groupoid homology ofS⋉ΩS\ltimes\Omega. The sequence of groupoidsS⋉ΩS\ltimes\Omega,S⋉E^S\ltimes\widehat{E}andS⋉{∞}S\ltimes\left\{\infty\right\}can be viewed as an independent co-resolution forS⋉ΩS\ltimes\Omega. Let us analyse the long exact sequence in groupoid homology arising from the decompositionE^=Ω∐{∞}\widehat{E}=\Omega\amalg\left\{\infty\right\}:(6.8)⋯→H∗+1⁡(S⋉{∞})→H∗⁡(S⋉Ω)→H∗⁡(S⋉E^)→H∗⁡(S⋉{∞})→H∗−1⁡(S⋉Ω)→⋯\cdots\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*+1}(S\ltimes\left\{\infty\right\})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\Omega)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\widehat{E})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\left\{\infty\right\})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*-1}(S\ltimes\Omega)\to\cdots

Groupoid homology ofH∗⁡(S⋉E^)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\widehat{E})is given by Corollary4.11as follows:H∗⁡(S⋉E^)≅H∗⁡(Π)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\widehat{E})\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(\Pi)

because the actionS↷E×S\mathbin{\curvearrowright}E^{\times}only has one orbit, and the corresponding isotropy group isΠ\Pi. The groupoidS⋉{∞}S\ltimes\left\{\infty\right\}is already discrete, and we have an isomorphismS⋉{∞}≅A⋊ΠS\ltimes\left\{\infty\right\}\cong A\rtimes\Pi. Plugging all this into (6.8), and using the isomorphismH∗⁡(G)≅H∗⁡(S⋉Ω)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(G)\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(S\ltimes\Omega)induced by the canonical inclusionG↪S⋉ΩG\hookrightarrow S\ltimes\Omega, we obtain the following long exact sequence:(6.9)⋯→H∗+1⁡(A⋊Π)→H∗⁡(G)→H∗⁡(Π)→H∗⁡(A⋊Π)→H∗−1⁡(G)→⋯\cdots\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*+1}(A\rtimes\Pi)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(G)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(\Pi)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(A\rtimes\Pi)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*-1}(G)\to\cdots

Moreover, it is straightforward to see that the mapH∗⁡(Π)→H∗⁡(A⋊Π)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(\Pi)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(A\rtimes\Pi)appearing in (6.9) is induced by the canonical inclusionΠ↪A⋊Π\Pi\hookrightarrow A\rtimes\Pi. Since the canonical projectionA⋊Π↠ΠA\rtimes\Pi\twoheadrightarrow\Piis a split for the canonical inclusionΠ↪A⋊Π\Pi\hookrightarrow A\rtimes\Pi, we obtain an isomorphismH∗⁡(G)≅H∗+1⁡(A⋊Π)/H∗+1⁡(Π)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(G)\cong\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*+1}(A\rtimes\Pi)/\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*+1}(\Pi).
Now consider the Lyndon–Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence (see e.g.[8, § VII.6])Hi⁡(Π,Hj⁡(A,ℤ))⇒Hi+j⁡(A⋊Π).\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{i}(\Pi,\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{j}(A,\mathbb{Z}))\Rightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{i+j}(A\rtimes\Pi).

The canonical inclusionΠ↪A⋊Π\Pi\hookrightarrow A\rtimes\Piand its splitA⋊Π↠ΠA\rtimes\Pi\twoheadrightarrow\Piallow us to viewH∗⁡(Π)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(\Pi)as a direct summand ofH∗⁡(A⋊Π)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(A\rtimes\Pi), and functoriality of the Lyndon–Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence enables us to quotient byH∗⁡(Π)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(\Pi)at each level of the spectral sequence. All in all, we obtain a spectral sequence converging toH∗⁡(G)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(G)of the formHp⁡(Π,Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ))⇒Hp+q⁡(G).\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{p}(\Pi,\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z}))\Rightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{p+q}(G).

Therefore, in this example, our independent co-resolution allows us to compute the groupoid homology ofGG. Here is a direct consequence:

## Corollary 6.13.


- (i)

IfΠ\Picontains a numberξ∈ℚ>0∖{1}\xi\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}\setminus\left\{1\right\}, thenH∗⁡(G)⊗ℤℚ=0\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(G)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=0for all∗*, and thus the corresponding topological full group𝑭​(G)\bm{F}(G)is rationally acyclic.
- (ii)

Assume thatΠ\Picontains a natural numberν>1\nu>1, and for all1≤r≤rk​A:-dimℚ​(A⊗ℤℚ)1\leq r\leq{\rm rk}\,\,A\coloneq{\rm dim}\,_{\mathbb{Q}}(A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}),AAis(νr−1)(\nu^{r}-1)-divisible (i.e., multiplication by(νr−1)(\nu^{r}-1)is an isomorphism onAA), which is the case if(νr−1)∈Π(\nu^{r}-1)\in\Pifor all1≤r≤rk​A1\leq r\leq{\rm rk}\,\,A. ThenH∗⁡(G)=0\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{*}(G)=0for all∗*, and thus the corresponding topological full group𝑭​(G)\bm{F}(G)is integrally acyclic.

## Proof.

(i) It suffices to show thatHp⁡(Π,Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ))⊗ℤℚ=0\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{p}(\Pi,\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=0for allp,q≥0p,q\geq 0. As group homology commutes with filtered colimits, it suffices to treat the case whereΠ\Piis finitely generated. In that case,Π\Piis free abelian. By the Elementary Divisor Theorem, there existsπ∈Π\pi\in\Pisuch thatΠ/⟨π⟩\Pi/\left\langle\pi\right\rangleis free abelian andξ∈⟨π⟩\xi\in\left\langle\pi\right\rangle. Letξ∗:Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ)⊗ℤℚ→Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ)⊗ℤℚ\xi_{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}be the map induced byξ\xi. Asξ≠1\xi\neq 1, we know thatid−ξ∗{\rm id}-\xi_{*}is an isomorphism onHq+1⁡(A,ℤ)⊗ℤℚ\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}. Asξ∈⟨π⟩\xi\in\left\langle\pi\right\rangle, there exists an integerϵ\epsilon, which we can assume to be positive (otherwise replaceπ\pibyπ−1\pi^{-1}), such thatξ=πϵ\xi=\pi^{\epsilon}. It follows thatid−ξ∗=id−(π∗)ϵ=(id−π∗)​((π∗)0+…+(π∗)ϵ−1){\rm id}-\xi_{*}={\rm id}-(\pi_{*})^{\epsilon}=({\rm id}-\pi_{*})((\pi_{*})^{0}+\dotso+(\pi_{*})^{\epsilon-1})onHq+1⁡(A,ℤ)⊗ℤℚ\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}. Sinceid−ξ∗{\rm id}-\xi_{*}is an isomorphism, it follows thatid−π∗{\rm id}-\pi_{*}is an isomorphism. HenceHn⁡(⟨π⟩,Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ))⊗ℤℚ=0\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{n}(\left\langle\pi\right\rangle,\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=0for alln≥0n\geq 0. Now the Lyndon–Hochschild–Serre spectral sequenceHm⁡(Π/⟨π⟩,Hn⁡(⟨π⟩,Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ)))⇒Hm+n⁡(Π,Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ))\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{m}(\Pi/\left\langle\pi\right\rangle,\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{n}(\left\langle\pi\right\rangle,\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z})))\Rightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{m+n}(\Pi,\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z}))

implies thatHp⁡(Π,Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ))⊗ℤℚ=0\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{p}(\Pi,\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=0for allp,q≥0p,q\geq 0, as desired. The statement about𝑭​(G)\bm{F}(G)follows from[37, Corollary C].

(ii) It suffices to show thatHp⁡(Π,Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ))=0\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{p}(\Pi,\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z}))=0for allp,q≥0p,q\geq 0. The proof is similar to the one in (i). The only difference is that, ifν∗:Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ)→Hq+1⁡(A,ℤ)\nu_{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z})is the map induced byν\nu, then our assumptions onAAimply thatid−ν∗{\rm id}-\nu_{*}is an isomorphism onHq+1⁡(A,ℤ)\operatorname{\mathrm{H}}_{q+1}(A,\mathbb{Z}). In other words, there is no need to rationalize. The statement about𝑭​(G)\bm{F}(G)follows from[37, Corollary D]becauseGGis purely infinite minimal and the unit space ofGGis homeomorphic to the Cantor space (see[68, § 7], in particular[68, Lemmas 7.2.4 and 7.2.7]).
∎

## Appendix AEquivariant étale correspondences to equivariantC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondences

For K-theoretic considerations we shall heavily utilise the construction of aC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondenceC∗⁡(Ω):C∗⁡(G)→C∗⁡(H)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\Omega)\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(G)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(H)from an étale correspondenceΩ:G→H\Omega\colon G\to H. Proper étale correspondences induce properC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondences, which induce maps in (K)K-theory. We will often use étale correspondences as models for these maps in (K)K-theory. We also get aC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondence at the reduced level when the left action is free, and therefore for any étale correspondence built from an inverse semigroup equivariant topological correspondence:

## Proposition A.1.

Then by construction we have⟨v,⟨ξ,a​ξ⟩​v⟩=∑t∈T⟨wt,a​wt⟩≤‖a‖λ​∑t∈T⟨wt,wt⟩=‖a‖λ​⟨v,⟨ξ,ξ⟩​v⟩≤‖a‖λ​‖ξ‖λ2​‖v‖22.∎\langle v,\langle\xi,a\xi\rangle v\rangle=\sum_{t\in T}\langle w_{t},aw_{t}\rangle\leq\|a\|_{\lambda}\sum_{t\in T}\langle w_{t},w_{t}\rangle=\|a\|_{\lambda}\langle v,\langle\xi,\xi\rangle v\rangle\leq\|a\|_{\lambda}\|\xi\|_{\lambda}^{2}\|v\|_{2}^{2}.\qed

We also need to utilise étale correspondences as models for maps in equivariant KK-theory, that is, morphisms in the equivariant Kasparov categoryKKG\operatorname{\mathrm{KK}}^{G}for some étale groupoidGG. ## Lemma A.3.

By upper semicontinuity of the norm we have∥ξs​(zi)−ai∥<ϵ\lVert\xi_{s(z_{i})}-a_{i}\rVert<\epsilonfor sufficiently largeii, and hence also∥zi.ξs​(zi)−zi.ai∥<ϵ\lVert z_{i}.\xi_{s(z_{i})}-z_{i}.a_{i}\rVert<\epsilon. It follows by[76, Proposition C.20]thatzi.ai→z.az_{i}.a_{i}\to z.a.

## Proposition A.5.

The construction of aGG-equivariantC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondenceC∗⁡(Ω):C∗⁡(J)→C∗⁡(K)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\Omega)\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(J)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(K)from aGG-equivariant étale correspondenceΩ:J→K\Omega\colon J\to Kdescribed above respects composition and identities up toGG-equivariant isomorphism.

We take the chance here to highlight a particular special case of the above construction. LetGGbe an étale groupoid and letXXandYYbe locally compact HausdorffGG-spaces. AGG-equivariant topological correspondenceZ:X→YZ\colon X\to Yis a locally compact HausdorffGG-spaceZZalong with aGG-equivariant local homeomorphismσ:Z→Y\sigma\colon Z\to Yand aGG-equivariant mapρ:Z→X\rho\colon Z\to X. We obtain aGG-equivariantC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondenceC∗⁡(Z):C0​(X)→C0​(Y)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(Z)\colon C_{0}(X)\to C_{0}(Y). In[58],ZZis called aspanandZ↦C∗⁡(Z)Z\mapsto\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(Z)Atiyah transfer.

LetGGbe an étale groupoid and letKKbe an étaleGG-groupoid. ThenG⋉KG\ltimes Kcarries the structure of an étale groupoid with unit spaceK0K^{0}, withs​(g,k)=s​(k)s(g,k)=s(k),r(g,k)=r(g.k)r(g,k)=r(g.k)and multiplication given by(g1.k1)(g2,k2)=(g1g2,(g2−1.k1)k2)(g_{1}.k_{1})(g_{2},k_{2})=(g_{1}g_{2},(g_{2}^{-1}.k_{1})k_{2})for composable pairs.

## Proposition A.6.

LetGGbe an étale groupoid and letKKbe an étaleGG-groupoid. Write𝒜→G0\mathcal{A}\to G^{0}for theGG-Banach bundle associated toC∗⁡(K)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(K). Then there is a∗*-isomorphismΦ:C∗⁡(G⋉K)→G⋉C∗⁡(K)\Phi\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(G\ltimes K)\to G\ltimes\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(K)given atξ∈Cc​(G⋉K)\xi\in C_{c}(G\ltimes K)byΦ:Cc​(G⋉K)\displaystyle\Phi\colon C_{c}(G\ltimes K)→Γc​(G,s∗​𝒜)\displaystyle\to\Gamma_{c}(G,s^{*}\mathcal{A})ξ\displaystyle\xi↦[g↦[k↦ξ​(g,k)]]\displaystyle\mapsto[g\mapsto[k\mapsto\xi(g,k)]]

## Proof.

We first argue thatΦ\Phias above is well-defined onCc​(G⋉K)C_{c}(G\ltimes K)- by linearity it suffices to considerξ∈Cc​(U;K)=Cc​({(g,k)∈G⋉K∣g∈U})\xi\in C_{c}(U;K)=C_{c}(\{(g,k)\in G\ltimes K\mid g\in U\})for each open bisectionU⊆GU\subseteq G. Then, for fixedg∈Ug\in U, the functionk↦ξ​(g,k)k\mapsto\xi(g,k)is an element ofCc​(τ−1​(s​(g)))⊆As​(g)C_{c}(\tau^{-1}(s(g)))\subseteq A_{s(g)}. To see thatg↦[k↦ξ​(g,k)]:U→s∗​𝒜g\mapsto[k\mapsto\xi(g,k)]\colon U\to s^{*}\mathcal{A}is continuous, fixg0∈Ug_{0}\in Uand pickγ∈Cc​(U)\gamma\in C_{c}(U)withγ=1\gamma=1on an open neighbourhoodV⊆UV\subseteq Uofg0g_{0}. The functionη:K\displaystyle\eta\colon K→ℂ\displaystyle\to\mathbb{C}k\displaystyle k↦∑g∈Gτ​(k)γ​(g)​ξ​(g,k).\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{g\in G_{\tau(k)}}\gamma(g)\xi(g,k).

is continuous and compactly supported. We computeη​(k)=ξ​(g,k)\eta(k)=\xi(g,k)forg∈Vg\in Vandk∈τ−1​(s​(g))k\in\tau^{-1}(s(g)). It follows thatg↦[k↦ξ​(g,k)]=ηs​(g)g\mapsto[k\mapsto\xi(g,k)]=\eta_{s(g)}is continuous onVV. We obtain a∗*-homomorphismCc​(G⋉K)→Γc​(G,s∗​𝒜)C_{c}(G\ltimes K)\to\Gamma_{c}(G,s^{*}\mathcal{A})which by universality extends to a∗*-homomorphismΦ:C∗⁡(G⋉K)→G⋉C∗⁡(K)\Phi\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(G\ltimes K)\to G\ltimes\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(K).

For an open bisectionU⊆GU\subseteq G, the inclusionΦ:Cc​(U;K)↪Γc​(U,s∗​𝒜)\Phi\colon C_{c}(U;K)\hookrightarrow\Gamma_{c}(U,s^{*}\mathcal{A})is isometric by theC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-identity. Indeed, forξ∈Cc​(U;K)\xi\in C_{c}(U;K), we haveΦ​(ξ)∗​Φ​(ξ)∈Γc​(s​(U),𝒜)⊆Cc​(K)⊆C∗⁡(K)\Phi(\xi)^{*}\Phi(\xi)\in\Gamma_{c}(s(U),\mathcal{A})\subseteq C_{c}(K)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(K). The open inclusionK↪G⋉KK\hookrightarrow G\ltimes Kinduces a∗*-homomorphismC∗⁡(K)→C∗⁡(G⋉K)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(K)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(G\ltimes K)which sendsΦ​(ξ)∗​Φ​(ξ)\Phi(\xi)^{*}\Phi(\xi)toξ∗​ξ\xi^{*}\xi, and thus∥Φ​(ξ)∥≥∥ξ∥\lVert\Phi(\xi)\rVert\geq\lVert\xi\rVert. The image is dense becauseΓc​(U,s∗​𝒜)\Gamma_{c}(U,s^{*}\mathcal{A})carries the supremum norm overUUand the image in each fibre(s∗​𝒜)g(s^{*}\mathcal{A})_{g}forg∈Ug\in Ucontains the dense subsetCc(τ−1(s(g))C_{c}(\tau^{-1}(s(g)).

Supposeπ:Cc​(G⋉K)→ℬ​(ℋ)\pi\colon C_{c}(G\ltimes K)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})is a∗*-representation. Then we get a unique extensionπU:Γc​(U,s∗​𝒜)→ℬ​(ℋ)\pi_{U}\colon\Gamma_{c}(U,s^{*}\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})for eachUU. These subspaces spanΓc​(G,s∗​𝒜)\Gamma_{c}(G,s^{*}\mathcal{A}), and we claim the extensionπ~:∑i=1nξi↦∑i=1nπUi​(ξi):Γc​(G,s∗​𝒜)→ℬ​(ℋ)\widetilde{\pi}\colon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{U_{i}}(\xi_{i})\colon\Gamma_{c}(G,s^{*}\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})ofπ\pitoΓc​(G,s∗​𝒜)\Gamma_{c}(G,s^{*}\mathcal{A})is well-defined, whereξi∈Γc​(Ui,s∗​𝒜)\xi_{i}\in\Gamma_{c}(U_{i},s^{*}\mathcal{A})for some open bisectionUiU_{i}. Indeed, if∑i=1nξi=0\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}=0, considerV={g∈U1∣f​(g)≠0}⊆U1V=\{g\in U_{1}\mid f(g)\neq 0\}\subseteq U_{1}, which is open and Hausdorff and covered by{Ui∩U1}i≥2\{U_{i}\cap U_{1}\}_{i\geq 2}. Via a partition of unity, we may findηi∈Γc​(Ui∩U1,s∗​𝒜)\eta_{i}\in\Gamma_{c}(U_{i}\cap U_{1},s^{*}\mathcal{A})withξ1=∑i=2nηi\xi_{1}=\sum_{i=2}^{n}\eta_{i}, so that∑i=2n(ξi+ηi)=0\sum_{i=2}^{n}(\xi_{i}+\eta_{i})=0. By induction we can assume∑i=2nπUi​(ξi+ηi)=0\sum_{i=2}^{n}\pi_{U_{i}}(\xi_{i}+\eta_{i})=0, but sinceπUi​(ηi)=πU1​(ηi)\pi_{U_{i}}(\eta_{i})=\pi_{U_{1}}(\eta_{i}), we conclude∑i=1nπUi​(ξi)=0\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{U_{i}}(\xi_{i})=0. The extensionπ~\widetilde{\pi}is by construction a∗*-representation, henceΦ\Phiis isometric and thus a∗*-isomorphism.
∎

LetΩ:J→K\Omega\colon J\to Kbe aGG-equivariant étale correspondence. Then there is an étale correspondenceG⋉Ω:G⋉J→G⋉KG\ltimes\Omega\colon G\ltimes J\to G\ltimes Kwith underlying spaceG⋉Ω=G×G0ΩG\ltimes\Omega=G\times_{G^{0}}\Omega, anchor maps given byρ(g,ω)=ρ(g.ω)\rho(g,\omega)=\rho(g.\omega)andσ​(g,ω)=σ​(ω)\sigma(g,\omega)=\sigma(\omega)and actions given by(g1,j).(g2,ω)=(g1g2,(g2−1.j).ω)(g_{1},j).(g_{2},\omega)=(g_{1}g_{2},(g_{2}^{-1}.j).\omega)and(g1,ω).(g2,k)=(g1g2,(g2−1.ω).k)(g_{1},\omega).(g_{2},k)=(g_{1}g_{2},(g_{2}^{-1}.\omega).k). This construction is compatible with composition: given anotherGG-equivariant étale correspondenceΛ:K→L\Lambda\colon K\to L, there is a canonical isomorphism(G⋉Ω)×G⋉K(G⋉Λ)→G⋉(Ω×KΛ)(G\ltimes\Omega)\times_{G\ltimes K}(G\ltimes\Lambda)\to G\ltimes(\Omega\times_{K}\Lambda)given by[(g1,ω),(g2,λ)]↦(g1g2,[g2−1.ω,λ])[(g_{1},\omega),(g_{2},\lambda)]\mapsto(g_{1}g_{2},[g_{2}^{-1}.\omega,\lambda]).

## Proposition A.7.

LetGGbe an étale groupoid and letΩ:J→K\Omega\colon J\to Kbe aGG-equivariant topological correspondence. ThenG⋉C∗⁡(Ω):G⋉C∗⁡(J)→G⋉C∗⁡(K)G\ltimes\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\Omega)\colon G\ltimes\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(J)\to G\ltimes\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(K)is isomorphic toC∗⁡(G⋉Ω):C∗⁡(G⋉J)→C∗⁡(G⋉K)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(G\ltimes\Omega)\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(G\ltimes J)\to\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(G\ltimes K).

## Proof.

Consider theGG-Banach bundleℰ→G0\mathcal{E}\to G^{0}associated to theGG-HilbertC∗⁡(K)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(K)-moduleC∗⁡(Ω)\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(\Omega), whose fibre atw∈G0w\in G^{0}is a completion ofCc​(τΩ−1​(w))C_{c}(\tau_{\Omega}^{-1}(w)). By the same argument as in PropositionA.6, the mapΦΩ:Cc​(G⋉Ω)\displaystyle\Phi_{\Omega}\colon C_{c}(G\ltimes\Omega)→Γc​(G,s∗​ℰ)\displaystyle\to\Gamma_{c}(G,s^{*}\mathcal{E})ξ\displaystyle\xi↦[g↦[ω↦ξ​(g,ω)]]\displaystyle\mapsto[g\mapsto[\omega\mapsto\xi(g,\omega)]]

is well-defined. Consider the∗*-isomorphismsΦJ:C∗⁡(G⋉J)→G⋉C∗⁡(J)\Phi_{J}\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(G\ltimes J)\to G\ltimes\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(J)andΦK:C∗⁡(G⋉K)→G⋉C∗⁡(K)\Phi_{K}\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(G\ltimes K)\to G\ltimes\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}(K)from PropositionA.6. Expanding terms shows that fora∈Cc​(G⋉J)a\in C_{c}(G\ltimes J),b∈Cc​(G⋉K)b\in C_{c}(G\ltimes K)andξ,η∈Cc​(G⋉Ω)\xi,\eta\in C_{c}(G\ltimes\Omega), we haveΦJ​(a).ΦΩ​(ξ)\displaystyle\Phi_{J}(a).\Phi_{\Omega}(\xi)=ΦΩ(a.ξ),\displaystyle=\Phi_{\Omega}(a.\xi),ΦΩ​(ξ).ΦK​(b)\displaystyle\Phi_{\Omega}(\xi).\Phi_{K}(b)=ΦΩ(ξ.b),\displaystyle=\Phi_{\Omega}(\xi.b),⟨ΦΩ​(η),ΦΩ​(ξ)⟩\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{\Omega}(\eta),\Phi_{\Omega}(\xi)\rangle=ΦK​(⟨η,ξ⟩).\displaystyle=\Phi_{K}(\langle\eta,\xi\rangle).

Moreover,ΦΩ\Phi_{\Omega}has dense image by the same argument as in PropositionA.6; for each open bisectionU⊆GU\subseteq Gthe image ofCc​(U;Ω)C_{c}(U;\Omega)inΓc​(U,s∗​ℰ)\Gamma_{c}(U,s^{*}\mathcal{E})is dense because the latter carries the supremum norm overUUand the image in each fibre is dense. We conclude thatΦΩ\Phi_{\Omega}extends to an isomorphism ofC∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-correspondences.
∎

In summary, we have constructed the following diagram of functors, and shown that it commutes.E´​taleCorrG{{{\mathrm{\acute{E}taleCorr}}^{G}}}C∗⁡CorrG{{{\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}\mathrm{Corr}}^{G}}}E´​taleCorr{{{\mathrm{\acute{E}taleCorr}}}}C∗⁡Corr.{{{\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}\mathrm{Corr}}.}}C∗\scriptstyle{\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}}G⋉−\scriptstyle{G\ltimes-}G⋉−\scriptstyle{G\ltimes-}C∗\scriptstyle{\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}}

Here,E´​taleCorrG{\mathrm{\acute{E}taleCorr}}^{G}is the category of étaleGG-groupoids, with morphisms given by isomorphism classes ofGG-equivariant étale correspondences, andC∗⁡CorrG{{\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}\mathrm{Corr}}^{G}}is the analogous category ofGG-C∗\operatorname{\mathrm{C}^{\ast}}-algebras. Although there most likely exists a22-categorical refinement of this, we have no need for it in this paper.
